## 💰 Making Money & Side Hustles

> Turn AI into a money printer: freelance gigs, bargain hunting, bookkeeping

<a id="skill-001"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>001. Auto-Reply Script Library for Xianyu Chats</b> —— Answering "still available?" and "lowest price?" a dozen times a day on Xianyu…</summary>

Answering "still available?" and "lowest price?" a dozen times a day on Xianyu (China's secondhand marketplace) — your fingers go numb from copy-pasting, and a slow reply can cost you the sale.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send screenshots of your 10 most-asked questions to Muse and have it compile a standard reply library.

2. Tell Muse: "When someone asks about price, timeline, or after-sales, pick the best line from the library for me so I can copy and send it."

3. Each week, feed it the new weird questions you get — the library keeps getting better.

**Real talk:** Xianyu doesn't give Muse an API, so it can't auto-reply for you. It can only prep the scripts — you still have to hit send yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-002"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>002. Cold Email Opening Lines</b> —— Dozens of cold emails sent into the void; the subject line gets deleted…</summary>

Dozens of cold emails sent into the void; the subject line gets deleted at a glance, and you have no idea which sentence went wrong.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give it a one-line product pitch plus your target customer profile, and have it write 5 opening lines from 5 different angles.

2. Tell Muse: "Use this opening line to draft a pitch under 150 words, and end with one specific question."

3. Send the unanswered versions back and have it critique them line by line, then test which rewrite gets more replies next round.

**Real talk:** Muse can't blast emails for you. It only writes the email — you hit send.

</details>

<a id="skill-003"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>003. Quote Sheets That Stop Price Mix-Ups</b> —— Quotes buried in chat history; a client asks "what did you quote me last time?"…</summary>

Quotes buried in chat history; a client asks "what did you quote me last time?" and you dig forever — still afraid you'll mix up the price.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse screenshots of all your quoted chats or your quote sheets, and have it compile a master quote table by client.

2. Tell Muse: "Client A is asking about product X — give me my last quoted price plus a follow-up line."

3. After every price change, tell it the new price and the table updates in real time. From then on, quote from the table and never mix up a price again.

**Real talk:** Muse can handle all three steps — you just feed it screenshots. But the final number you send the client is your call; if you quote the wrong price, it's on you.

</details>

<a id="skill-004"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>004. Subscription Auto-Renewal Cleanup</b> —— App memberships auto-renew and you only notice when the money's gone…</summary>

App memberships auto-renew and you only notice when the money's gone — a few hundred dollars a year thrown away.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Connect your bank account and have it pull every automatic charge, listing each subscription's name and amount.

2. Tell Muse: "Flag every subscription I haven't used in three months and rank them by annual fee, highest first."

3. Cancel them in each app following the list, then have it set a monthly reminder to alert you the moment a new charge appears.

**Real talk:** Cancelling requires your own tap — Muse only tells you which ones to cancel. It can't press the confirm button in your banking app.

</details>

<a id="skill-005"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>005. Price Your Used Stuff Without Leaving Money on the Table</b> —— Listing stuff for sale and pricing by gut feel…</summary>

Listing stuff for sale and pricing by gut feel: price too high and nobody asks, price too low and you bleed money.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse a photo of the item's brand, model, and condition, plus your bottom-line price.

2. Tell Muse: "Search recent sold prices for the same item on the resale market and give me a pricing range that'll move it fast."

3. List at its price range. If it hasn't sold in a week, come back and ask: "Should I drop the price, and by how much?"

**Real talk:** Muse handles all the price research. But listing, changing prices, and haggling with buyers all happen in the app with your own taps — it can't log into your Xianyu.

</details>

<a id="skill-006"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>006. Paid Course Outline, Fast-Tracked</b> —— You've got the know-how to sell a course, but writing the outline and the sales…</summary>

You've got the know-how to sell a course, but the thought of writing the outline and the sales copy has stalled you for three months.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "I want to turn my XX skill into a paid column — first, list 10 painful topics students would actually pay for."

2. Pick one topic and have it expand into a course outline, with each lesson summed up in one line: what you'll be able to do after it.

3. Have it draft a free sample chapter from the outline; you just fix mistakes and add your own cases — Muse handles the whole skeleton.

**Real talk:** Muse can do all three steps; you just pick and edit. But whether the course sells depends on whether you actually know your stuff — that's one thing it can't do for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-007"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>007. Automatic Side-Hustle Ledger</b> —— Side income coming in through several channels, WeChat and Alipay all mixed…</summary>

Side income coming in through several channels, WeChat and Alipay all mixed together — by month's end you can't even say how much you earned.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Export your monthly statements from WeChat and Alipay, send them to Muse, and have it sort everything into an income-vs-expense table.

2. Tell Muse: "Pull out just the side-hustle income and figure out my net earnings this month."

3. On the 1st of each month, drop the new statements in and the ledger extends itself — at year-end you get the full-year income curve.

**Real talk:** WeChat and Alipay don't offer APIs, so you export the statements by hand and send them over. Muse handles the sorting and categorizing from there.

</details>

<a id="skill-008"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>008. Wishlist Price-Drop Alerts</b> —— You check the price of something you want every day, miss the drop…</summary>

You check the price of something you want every day, miss the drop, and then it goes on sale the day after you buy — maddening.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "Keep an eye on this product — check the lowest price across the web once a day, and ping me the moment it drops below X dollars."

2. Once it sets up the scheduled task, you can forget about it — the price-drop alert lands on its own.

3. When the alert comes, don't buy immediately. Ask first: "Is this a genuine all-time low or a fake markdown?" — it'll check the price history.

**Real talk:** Muse handles the first two steps end to end and calls you on time when prices drop. But step 3 — the actual purchase — is your tap. It can't pay for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-009"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>009. Side-Hustle Idea Bank, Filtered</b> —— Side-hustle ideas pop into your head, get jotted in a note app…</summary>

Side-hustle ideas pop into your head, get jotted in a note app, then vanish — when you actually want to start something, you can't recall a single one.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send each side-hustle idea to Muse as it comes; it files it in your idea bank and adds a note on why it might work.

2. Once you have 10, say: "Rank these by startup cost and how fast they can make money, and cut the 3 weakest."

3. Before committing, have it expand the winner into a minimum viable plan: what to do first, and how soon you can tell whether it makes money.

**Real talk:** Muse can handle all three steps — you just toss it ideas. But turning a plan into reality takes your own legwork; it can't go run errands for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-010"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>010. Never Forget a Client Follow-Up</b> —— A client says "let's talk in two weeks," you turn around and forget…</summary>

A client says "let's talk in two weeks," you turn around and forget — and by the time you remember, they've already gone with someone else.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. After every client conversation, send Muse "who, what we talked about, when to follow up next" and it logs it on your calendar.

2. Tell Muse: "Every morning at 9, remind me which clients to follow up with today, with the key points from our last chat."

3. After each follow-up, report the result back — it updates the status and pushes the next follow-up date out automatically.

**Real talk:** Muse handles the logging and reminding. But step 3's follow-up call is yours to make — it can't chat with your clients; closing the deal is on you.

</details>

<a id="skill-011"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>011. Decode the Key Clauses in Contracts</b> —— The client's contract is a dozen pages; your eyes glaze over at…</summary>

The client's contract is a dozen pages; your eyes glaze over at "liability for breach" — and signing it blind is the easiest way to get burned.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the contract PDF to Muse and have it flag the three critical spots: payment terms, breach clauses, and termination conditions.

2. Tell Muse: "In plain English, tell me the three clauses in this contract that hurt me most."

3. After signing, store your invoice details with it too — next time you need an invoice, just ask Muse instead of digging through chat history.

**Real talk:** Muse handles the reading and filing. But the signature has to be yours — and if the contract has real landmines, get a lawyer; Muse's word isn't legal advice.

</details>

<a id="skill-012"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>012. Reconcile Payments Across Channels</b> —— Money flows in through WeChat, Alipay, and bank cards…</summary>

Money flows in through WeChat, Alipay, and bank cards — reconciliation is all in your head, and a missing payment would slip right by.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Export your payment records from each channel and send them to Muse; it sorts them into one master table by client and project.

2. Tell Muse: "Mark what's paid and what's unpaid, and give me a list of clients who owe me money."

3. Send it each week's new payments to keep the table current; at month's end just ask: "Who hasn't settled up this month?"

**Real talk:** The payment channels have no open APIs, so you export the records by hand. Muse only does the sorting and reconciling — the actual nudge to pay still comes from you.

</details>

<a id="skill-013"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>013. Track Your Job Applications</b> —— You've chatted with dozens of recruiters on hiring apps…</summary>

You've chatted with dozens of recruiters on hiring apps — who replied, who asked for your resume, which interviews are scheduled — it's all a blur.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send it your resume and target role, and have it tailor a version for that position.

2. Tell Muse: "Track each company in a table: recruiter name, where the conversation stands, and my next step."

3. Update the table after each chat, and before an interview have it draft 5 likely questions based on the job description.

**Real talk:** Hiring apps don't offer APIs — screenshot the chats and send them over. Applying and replying are still your own taps.

</details>

<a id="skill-014"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>014. Track Returns and Shipping Insurance</b> —— You bought a pile of stuff; which items should be returned…</summary>

You bought a pile of stuff; which items should be returned, which shipping insurance is about to expire — you can't keep track, and the return window closes.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse screenshots of your order pages and have it list every order that's returnable with insurance about to expire.

2. Tell Muse: "Every evening, remind me to check my pending deliveries and flag any problems."

3. When you need a return, have it write you a script for negotiating with customer service — copy, paste, and the return goes smoothly.

**Real talk:** Shopping apps don't offer APIs — you screenshot the orders. Muse only organizes and reminds; filing the return request is your own tap.

</details>

<a id="skill-015"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>015. Build Client Profiles on WeChat</b> —— You've got hundreds of clients on WeChat — who is who, what you talked…</summary>

You've got hundreds of clients on WeChat — who is who, what you talked about — all held together by avatars and nicknames in your memory.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send it screenshots of key client chats and have it build a mini-profile for each: needs, budget, personality.

2. Tell Muse: "Mr. Wang's renewal is coming up — remind me three days early, and give me a graceful greeting to send him."

3. After every chat, toss the new info to Muse; the profile updates itself, and you review it before your next conversation.

**Real talk:** WeChat has no open API — you screenshot the chats. The messages themselves still have to be sent by your own hand.

</details>

<a id="skill-016"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>016. Review Your Side-Hustle Income Goals</b> —— You've been shouting "10K a month" for half a year…</summary>

You've been shouting "10K a month" for half a year, but you have no real sense of how far you are from the target.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "My side-hustle goal is 8,000 a month by year-end — break it into monthly milestones and track them."

2. Report your weekly income and progress; it updates the progress bar so the gap is obvious at a glance.

3. At month's end, have it write a review: which income stream beat expectations, which dragged, and which to cut next month.

**Real talk:** The numbers in steps 1 and 2 come from you — it can't calculate income you never told it about. And no matter how good its review is, which business to cut is your call.

</details>

<a id="skill-017"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>017. Three-Tier Quoting for Freelance Gigs</b> —— A client asks "how much?" and you blurt out a number…</summary>

A client asks "how much?" and you blurt out a number — after the work's done you realize your hourly rate is worse than a bubble-tea shop's.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. List the gigs you've done and have Muse calculate your baseline hourly rate from "time × difficulty."

2. Tell it: "Using that rate, build me three pricing tiers: basic, standard, and rush."

3. From now on, when a client asks for a price, send the tier sheet — the rush tier is specifically for "I need it tomorrow."

**Real talk:** Muse does the math, but the final number you send is your call. Don't take loss-making gigs.

</details>

<a id="skill-018"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>018. Three Lines to Hold Your Price</b> —— A client says "make it cheaper" and your mouth goes soft…</summary>

A client says "make it cheaper" and your mouth goes soft — you knock off 30% and want to slap yourself afterward.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your most common haggling scenarios and have it prep three responses: hold, concede, walk away.

2. Tell it: "The client says it's too expensive — give me a line that doesn't cheapen me but keeps them interested."

3. When you do concede, cut the deliverables, not the price — have Muse write the wording for you.

**Real talk:** Muse stocks the scripts, but you're the one hitting send at the negotiation table. Don't break your own bottom line first.

</details>

<a id="skill-019"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>019. Final-Payment Reminder Calendar</b> —— The work's done but the final payment drags…</summary>

The work's done but the final payment drags. Chasing it feels awkward; not chasing it hurts.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse each invoice's remaining balance and due date, and have it build a payment calendar.

2. Tell it: "If a payment is three days overdue, remind me — and give me a tactful collection line."

3. Every time a payment lands, tell Muse so it marks it paid; at month's end have it summarize: who dragged, and for how long.

**Real talk:** It can only remind you to chase — the money won't fly to you on its own. You have to send the collection message yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-020"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>020. Deliverables Acceptance Checklist</b> —— After delivery the client says "this isn't what I asked for"…</summary>

After delivery the client says "this isn't what I asked for" — three rounds of rework later, the final payment is still hanging.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. The moment you take the gig, toss the requirements to Muse: "Turn these into a delivery acceptance checklist."

2. Self-check against the list before delivery — fill any gaps, and send the checklist along with the deliverables.

3. When the client nitpicks, negotiate against the checklist: in-scope items get fixed, out-of-scope items cost extra.

**Real talk:** Muse writes the checklist, but the client has to sign off on acceptance themselves — it can't sign for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-021"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>021. Turn Vague Requests Into Quotable Proposals</b> —— The client says "just make it look nice" and you work yourself…</summary>

The client says "just make it look nice" and you work yourself to the bone — and they're still not happy.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the client's exact words and have it split them into "items I can quote" and "questions I must ask."

2. Tell it: "Based on these questions, give me 5 questions that won't make me sound like an amateur."

3. After the client answers, send the answers back and have it produce a "requirements confirmation" — get the client's nod before you start.

**Real talk:** Muse writes the confirmation, but getting the client's nod is on you — it can't sit in the meeting for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-022"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>022. Turn Praise Screenshots Into Trust Badges</b> —— Every compliment a client ever paid you is buried in chat history…</summary>

Every compliment a client ever paid you is buried in chat history — a new client asks "are you legit?" and you're left scrolling forever.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send all your praise screenshots to Muse and have it file them by service type.

2. Tell it: "A new client is asking if I'm reliable — pick the 3 most convincing reviews and combine them into one line."

3. Each month, have it distill one signature selling point from the reviews and update your self-introduction.

**Real talk:** You have to collect the praise one screenshot at a time — Muse can't conjure clients out of thin air. Your service is the source.

</details>

<a id="skill-023"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>023. How Much to Add for Invoices</b> —— A client asks for an invoice and you casually say "add 3 points"…</summary>

A client asks for an invoice and you casually say "add 3 points" — then end up paying the tax out of your own pocket.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse how you issue invoices and have it work out your real tax burden as a percentage.

2. Tell it: "Based on that rate, give me a one-liner I can say at quote time to explain invoicing clearly."

3. From now on, write "tax-inclusive / tax-exclusive price" right on your quote sheet — don't wait for the client to ask.

**Real talk:** Muse crunches the tax rate, but issuing invoices and filing taxes happen in the tax system with your own hands — it can't touch that.

</details>

<a id="skill-024"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>024. One-Page Gig Contracts in Seconds</b> —— A gig worth a few grand feels too small for a contract…</summary>

A gig worth a few grand feels too small for a contract — but skipping it leaves you exposed if the other side ghosts you.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse the four essentials: what, how much, when it's due, and how revisions work.

2. Tell it: "Based on those four, draft me a one-page simple agreement."

3. Both sides confirm "agreed" on WeChat and keep the record; for big amounts, have a lawyer review it too.

**Real talk:** It can generate the template, but that's not legal advice — for large invoices, get a real lawyer to review.

</details>

<a id="skill-025"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>025. State Your Deposit Rules Up Front</b> —— You started work without a deposit, the client vanished mid-project…</summary>

You started work without a deposit, the client vanished mid-project — two weeks of work for nothing.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Have Muse work out, from your rates, "how much deposit covers my upfront costs."

2. Tell it: "Give me a message I can send clients before starting, explaining the deposit rules without bruising the relationship."

3. Write "deposit before work starts" into your order process — make it iron law.

**Real talk:** Muse sets the rules, but you have to be the one asking for the deposit — it can't collect money for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-026"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>026. Spot Fake Task-Scam Schemes</b> —— A part-time gig group promises "300 a day, just tap your phone"…</summary>

A part-time gig group promises "300 a day, just tap your phone" — and you only realize it's a scam after fronting 5,000.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Screenshot the scammer's pitch and send it to Muse; have it flag every suspicious line.

2. Tell it: "List the classic red flags of these part-time scams so I can check against them."

3. Remember the iron law: anyone who asks you to front money or pay a deposit first — block them immediately.

**Real talk:** It can help you see through the pitch, but once the money's transferred, it can't get it back. Stay sharp before you hit transfer.

</details>

<a id="skill-027"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>027. Vet Gig Listings Fast</b> —— Gig listings are everywhere and you can't tell which ones are legit…</summary>

Gig listings are everywhere and you can't tell which ones are legit — afraid of getting burned, afraid of missing out.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the job ad to Muse and have it score it on three axes: salary plausibility, upfront fees, and company info.

2. Tell it: "If they want money upfront or my ID card — instant death sentence, no hesitation."

3. For listings that pass the first screen, have it list 3 must-ask questions for the interview.

**Real talk:** It can only analyze what's on paper — verify the company yourself on Qichacha (China's corporate registry lookup). Don't be lazy about it.

</details>

<a id="skill-028"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>028. How to Price a Knowledge Course</b> —— The course is done and you're stuck: 99 feels like a loss…</summary>

The course is done and you're stuck: 99 feels like a loss, 999 feels like nobody will buy — two weeks of dithering and it's still not listed.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your course outline and target audience; have it find 3 comparable courses as price anchors.

2. Tell it: "Given my content volume, set me an early-bird price, a regular price, and a price-hike schedule."

3. Start with a small presale to validate; if it doesn't sell, have it analyze whether the problem is the price or the pitch.

**Real talk:** Muse advises on pricing, but the market decides whether people buy — you have to go sell that first unit yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-029"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>029. Follow Up With Course Students</b> —— The course sold, but students vanish after two lessons…</summary>

The course sold, but students vanish after two lessons — repeat purchases and referrals vanish with them.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse the student roster and progress; have it flag everyone who's gone 7+ days without studying.

2. Tell it: "Write a 'come back and learn' nudge for these students that isn't annoying."

3. For students who finish, have it generate a personal thank-you plus an advanced-course recommendation — and ask for a review while you're at it.

**Real talk:** Muse writes the reminder, but the DM has to come from you personally — students renew because of you, not the course.

</details>

<a id="skill-030"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>030. Livestream Sales Opening Script</b> —— First time going live and your mind goes blank on camera…</summary>

First time going live and your mind goes blank on camera — three minutes of product talk and you're out of words.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your product's selling points and have it write a word-for-word script for your first 3 minutes.

2. Tell it: "Now give me 5 viewer-retention lines: holding orders, giveaways, countdowns — the works."

3. After the stream, send it the data for a review: which segments kept viewers, which lost them, and fix it next time.

**Real talk:** Muse writes the script, but the mouth on camera is yours to train — it can't go live for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-031"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>031. Speedy quote sheets for agency work</b> —— A client asks "How much for monthly management?" — you mumble, deal dies.</summary>

A client asks "How much for monthly management?" — you mumble, deal dies.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. List what you can do, and have Muse split it into "basic, growth, full-service" tiers.

2. Tell it: "By each tier's workload, give me a quote sheet with line-item services."

3. When a client balks at the price, have it translate "expensive" into "extra results" — send the script directly.

**Real talk:** It builds the quote sheet; the pre-signing call is yours to make — trust is earned in conversation.

</details>

<a id="skill-032"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>032. ABC customer triage follow-ups</b> —— Client list keeps growing — who matters, who's whatever, all gut feel, big deal slips away.</summary>

Client list keeps growing — who matters, who's whatever, all gut feel, big deal slips away.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your client list and deal history; have it sort by "deal size × repeat likelihood" into A/B/C tiers.

2. Tell it: "A-tier clients get a check-in monthly — give me 3 non-salesy conversation starters."

3. Re-sort monthly: ask why for downgrades, send thanks for upgrades.

**Real talk:** It does the sorting; the dinners with A-tier clients are yours to show up for — relationships are built in person.

</details>

<a id="skill-033"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>033. Re-activate old customers</b> —— Old clients vanish after one job; winning a new one costs 3× keeping an old one…</summary>

Old clients vanish after one job; winning a new one costs 3× keeping an old one.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. List old clients with no orders in 3+ months, and tell Muse what the last project was.

2. Tell it: "For each one, write a personal check-in message — no mass-blast vibe."

3. For replies, have it prep follow-up scripts; for silence, try again in three months.

**Real talk:** It writes the messages; you send them one by one by hand — one mass blast and your old clients all run.

</details>

<a id="skill-034"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>034. Referral reward scripts</b> —— Clients say you're great, but nobody ever refers you new business.</summary>

Clients say you're great, but nobody ever refers you new business.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Have Muse design a "referral reward" around your ticket size: cash back, discount, or freebie.

2. Tell it: "At the end of delivery, send this script so the client naturally recommends me to friends."

3. When a referral lands, pay out the reward the same day, then have it write the thank-you.

**Real talk:** It designs the reward; you pay it out yourself — one delay and your reputation is toast.

</details>

<a id="skill-035"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>035. Do the math before your first day vending</b> —— Stocked up excitedly, sold a month, didn't even cover the stall fee.</summary>

Stocked up excitedly, sold a month, didn't even cover the stall fee.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Report purchase costs, stall fee, and transport to Muse; have it compute "how much to sell daily to break even."

2. Tell it: "On that break-even line, price three tiers: traffic driver, profit maker, image item."

3. Send it daily revenue after closing; review weekly — cut what doesn't sell.

**Real talk:** It crunches the numbers; you're the one paying for inventory — don't over-order the first time, start small.

</details>

<a id="skill-036"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>036. Are gig-platform tasks worth it?</b> —— Gig platforms full of tasks; the "¥500 for a 20-slide deck" trap, you've fallen for it more than once…</summary>

Gig platforms are full of tasks; the "¥500 for a 20-slide deck" trap, you've fallen for it more than once.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the task description and bounty to Muse; have it estimate "actual hours × your hourly rate."

2. Tell it: "Flag red anything below 80% of my rate — and check whether the employer has a bad-review history."

3. For the worth-it ones, have it draft a bid pitch that shows how you're different.

**Real talk:** It can only estimate on paper; you judge the employer yourself — read the reviews, not just the bounty.

</details>

<a id="skill-037"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>037. What is your time really worth</b> —— You take every job, grind 12 hours a day, and month-end math says a job would've paid better.</summary>

You take every job, grind 12 hours a day, and month-end math says a job would've paid better.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your monthly income target and available hours; have it compute your "floor hourly rate."

2. Tell it: "For jobs below this rate, write me a graceful decline template."

3. Log every job's hourly rate; at month-end, see which ones dragged your average down.

**Real talk:** It computes the rate; you supply the courage to turn down cheap jobs — don't go soft.

</details>

<a id="skill-038"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>038. Tax reminders for side income</b> —— Side income never declared — come tax season, one back-tax bill plus late fees, ouch.</summary>

Side income never declared — come tax season, one back-tax bill plus late fees, ouch.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your side-income types; have it spell out "which income must be declared, at what rate."

2. Tell it: "Remind me a month before the March annual filing, with a checklist of documents to prepare."

3. Have it log every large payment through the year — no scrambling at year-end.

**Real talk:** It looks up the policy; you file in person on the tax app — it can't sign for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-039"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>039. Blacklist for ghosting clients</b> —— Same client comes back under a new WeChat name, you don't recognize them, free work again.</summary>

Same client comes back under a new WeChat name, you don't recognize them, free work again.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Record the ghosting client's traits for Muse: WeChat name, phone, go-to phrases.

2. Tell it: "When a new client orders, cross-check against the blacklist — flag suspects."

3. Confirmed ghosts get refused outright; have it write a polite-but-firm refusal.

**Real talk:** It manages the list; you make the call on "suspects" — don't hurt a real customer.

</details>

<a id="skill-040"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>040. Don't fight bad reviews head-on</b> —— One bad review on your shop's front page, every inquiry runs off, you're furious and want to argue.</summary>

One bad review on your shop's front page, every inquiry runs off, you're furious and want to argue.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the review to Muse; have it judge: misunderstanding, real flaw, or malice.

2. Tell it: "For this situation, draft a public reply: own it first, then set the record straight — neither groveling nor defiant."

3. For privately solvable ones, have it write the DM script; ask for a follow-up review after resolving.

**Real talk:** It writes the reply; the private soothing is yours — sincerity can't be faked.

</details>

<a id="skill-041"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>041. Do the platform-fee math first</b> —— Platform gigs look high-paying; after commission + tax at withdrawal, you take home 30% less.</summary>

Platform gigs look high-paying; after commission + tax at withdrawal, you take home 30% less.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Screenshot the platform's fee rules for Muse; have it compute "what to quote to not lose money after fees."

2. Tell it: "Compare the three platforms I use — which has the highest take-home rate, in a table."

3. Quote by working backward from take-home; don't be fooled by the platform's listed prices.

**Real talk:** It crunches the rules; you check withdrawals and reconciliations in the platform backend yourself — it can't get in.

</details>

<a id="skill-042"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>042. Platform deposit refund reminders</b> —— You paid a deposit to join; six months after leaving you remembered — nearly gone for good.</summary>

You paid a deposit to join; six months after leaving you remembered — nearly gone for good.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse which platforms hold your deposits and how much; have it build a "deposit ledger."

2. Tell it: "Remind me a week before I leave/expire, with the refund application path."

3. Tell it when money lands to close the entry; for missing ones, have it write the chase script.

**Real talk:** It handles reminders; you file the refund application on the platform yourself — it can't click for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-043"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>043. Wholesale: compare three suppliers</b> —— You asked one supplier, then found the stall next door 15% cheaper — lost sleep…</summary>

You asked one supplier, then found the stall next door 15% cheaper — lost sleep.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Photograph the three quotes for Muse; have it total "unit price + shipping + payment terms."

2. Tell it: "Rank by total cost, marking each supplier's cheapest categories."

3. Before a long-term deal, have it list 5 haggling points — take them to the table.

**Real talk:** It does the comparison; you touch the goods in person — photos don't show quality.

</details>

<a id="skill-044"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>044. Flipping on Xianyu</b> —— You watch others flip for profit; you try it and get stuck holding the bag.</summary>

You watch others flip for profit; you try it and get stuck holding the bag.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your flip category; have it check "Xianyu historical price ranges."

2. Tell it: "On that range, set my buy cap and list price — keep 20% margin."

3. Inspect goods before listing; if unsold for two weeks, have it analyze: price or title problem.

**Real talk:** It checks the market; you're the one placing orders and paying — watch for fakes and pay-on-delivery scams yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-045"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>045. Group-buy host bookkeeping</b> —— Dozens of orders a day as a group-buy host; money and goods don't reconcile — you subsidize and don't even know.</summary>

Dozens of orders a day as a group-buy host; money and goods don't reconcile — you subsidize and don't even know.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send daily group-order screenshots to Muse; have it organize into "who ordered what, paid or not."

2. Tell it: "From this table, compute today's commission and advances — weekly rollup."

3. Have it flag and remind on anyone owing — script gentle but unambiguous.

**Real talk:** It keeps the books; collecting money and shipping goods is all you — it can't touch your stock.

</details>

<a id="skill-046"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>046. Quote sheets for PPT gigs</b> —— A friend referred a PPT job; you quoted ¥200, they went with the guy who quoted ¥800.</summary>

A friend referred a PPT job; you quoted ¥200, they went with the guy who quoted ¥800.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send past cases to Muse; have it price three tiers by "slides × design difficulty × rush."

2. Tell it: "Give me a quote template: revision limit included, extra rounds cost extra."

3. When a client sends requirements, have it estimate slides and hours first — then you quote the total, no loss.

**Real talk:** It sets the prices; you say "three revision rounds max" out loud to the client yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-047"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>047. Resume-writing service packages</b> —— You charge for resume help; the client thinks "it's just a few words" — why pay?</summary>

You charge for resume help; the client thinks "it's just a few words" — why pay?

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Break your service into packages: audit edition, rewrite edition, interview coaching; have Muse price three tiers.

2. Tell it: "Give me one paragraph explaining custom writing vs. templates — worth the price."

3. Attach a "change log" at delivery, showing the reason behind every edit.

**Real talk:** It designs the packages; you dig out the client's real story in person — it can't invent one.

</details>

<a id="skill-048"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>048. Home organizer pricing</b> —— You charge hourly for home organizing; clients think it's pricey, you think you're underpaid — lose-lose.</summary>

You charge hourly for home organizing; clients think it's pricey, you think you're underpaid — lose-lose.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your service flow; have it break pricing into "on-site assessment + organizing + restock supplies."

2. Tell it: "Price three bundles: one-bedroom, two-bedroom, villa."

3. Attach before/after cases to quotes — bundled pricing sells far better than hourly.

**Real talk:** It computes the quote; you're the one doing the physical work — don't underprice the labor.

</details>

<a id="skill-049"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>049. Patient-escort service scripts</b> —— You want the escort side gig, but don't know how to broach services and fees with families.</summary>

You want the escort side gig, but don't know how to broach services and fees with families.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. List your services; have Muse organize into "service list + fee schedule."

2. Tell it: "For the family's first inquiry, give me a trust-building opener."

3. After each escort, have it generate a "visit summary" — send to the family, instant professionalism.

**Real talk:** It writes the scripts; you're the one running around the hospital — care and thoroughness are the reputation.

</details>

<a id="skill-050"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>050. Revision boundaries for photo-retouch gigs</b> —— Eight revision rounds and the client still says "it doesn't feel right" — you want to smash the computer.</summary>

Eight revision rounds and the client still says "it doesn't feel right" — you want to smash the computer.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Before accepting, have Muse write your "revision rules": 2 free rounds, charged after.

2. Tell it: "When the client says it doesn't feel right, give me 5 questions to pin down the direction."

3. Have it log "what changed" each round — evidence if they keep backtracking.

**Real talk:** It sets the rules; you align on the "feeling" with the client in person — it can't see the image.

</details>

<a id="skill-051"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>051. Photoshoot quotes without the trap</b> —— You quoted a friendly price for a shoot: 6 hours plus retouching — hourly rate heartbreaking.</summary>

You quoted a friendly price for a shoot: 6 hours plus retouching — hourly rate heartbreaking.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse shoot duration, deliverables, retouch count; have it compute your "break-even quote."

2. Tell it: "Price three packages: travel shoot, portrait, commercial."

3. Spell out "overtime fee, extra-photo fee" in the quote — hard words upfront save feelings later.

**Real talk:** It computes the quote; you press the shutter — don't swallow the labor cost yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-052"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>052. Tutoring rates</b> —— You price tutoring by gut feel — teach great, still charge half of the college kid next door.</summary>

You price tutoring by gut feel — teach great, still charge half of the college kid next door.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your degree, subjects, score-improvement cases; have it benchmark local tutoring rates.

2. Tell it: "Price three formats: one-on-one, small group, online."

3. After the first session, have it generate a "learning report" — parents renew at double the rate.

**Real talk:** It gives pricing reference; you teach that trial lesson yourself — skill is the backbone of pricing.

</details>

<a id="skill-053"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>053. Translation gig workflow pack</b> —— You charge per word; the client stuffs in charts and jargon — two hours of free labor.</summary>

You charge per word; the client stuffs in charts and jargon — two hours of free labor.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the manuscript to Muse; have it count "words × jargon density" to estimate real hours.

2. Tell it: "On those hours, quote me a price with a jargon surcharge."

3. Attach a "glossary" at delivery — professionalism locked in, next time the client comes straight to you.

**Real talk:** It estimates hours; you own the quality — don't let machine-translation vibes trash your reputation.

</details>

<a id="skill-054"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>054. Where to pitch your writing</b> —— You wrote the piece but don't know where to pitch — a few silences and you're deflated.</summary>

You wrote the piece but don't know where to pitch — a few silences and you're deflated.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your writing niche; have it compile "matching publications/magazines + pay rates + requirements."

2. Tell it: "Rank by acceptance difficulty — pitch 3 practice ones first, then aim high-pay."

3. For rejections, have it analyze why, revise, and repitch — never send one piece to only one outlet.

**Real talk:** It finds the channels; you write every word yourself — it can't write for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-055"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>055. Voiceover audition prep checklist</b> —— You keep auditioning and losing; the client skips after the first line — you don't even know why.</summary>

You keep auditioning and losing; the client skips after the first line — you don't even know why.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the audition brief to Muse; have it break into "tone, pacing, emotion" prep items.

2. Tell it: "For this brief, write me 3 different-emotion audition openers."

3. Listen to your recording once; have it analyze "which segment is the problem."

**Real talk:** It writes the scripts; the voice is yours — daily vocal practice, don't expect it to do that for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-056"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>056. Survey hustling</b> —— Survey platforms everywhere; half an hour for ¥0.50 — time wasted.</summary>

Survey platforms everywhere; half an hour for ¥0.50 — time wasted.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send several survey platforms' rules to Muse; have it rank by "earnings per hour."

2. Tell it: "Only do the top 30% by price — below that number, not worth it."

3. Weekly revenue rollup; switch platforms when one starts "shrinking."

**Real talk:** It filters the platforms; you blacklist any survey group that asks you to pay first yourself — don't get greedy.

</details>

<a id="skill-057"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>057. Set a stop-loss for side gigs</b> —— Three months on a side gig, no money — can't bear to quit, don't dare invest more, just stuck.</summary>

Three months on a side gig, no money — can't bear to quit, don't dare invest more, just stuck.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your time and money invested; have it set a "stop-loss: how much longer, how much more."

2. Tell it: "Review against the stop-loss weekly — continue if on track, stop if not."

3. When you stop, have it summarize "what lesson this tuition bought" — not money wasted.

**Real talk:** It sets the stop-loss; you make the "stop" call yourself — no sentimentality.

</details>

<a id="skill-058"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>058. Invoice title management</b> —— A client wants an invoice; you ask for their title and tax ID every time — they think you're amateur.</summary>

A client wants an invoice; you ask for their title and tax ID every time — they think you're amateur.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send all past clients' titles and tax IDs to Muse; have it build a "title archive."

2. Tell it: "Check against the table before invoicing — auto-remind me to ask for missing tax IDs."

3. Collect titles at the first deal with a new client — file them once, done forever.

**Real talk:** It manages the archive; you do the invoicing in the tax system yourself — it can't get in.

</details>

<a id="skill-059"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>059. Client prepayment ledger</b> —— Several clients' prepayments mixed together — you overspend without knowing, month-end doesn't reconcile.</summary>

Several clients' prepayments mixed together — you overspend without knowing, month-end doesn't reconcile.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse each client's prepayment amount and consumed balance; have it build the ledger.

2. Tell it: "Remind me when a balance drops below 20% — time to ask the client to top up."

3. Send monthly statements to clients — it crunches the numbers, you send them.

**Real talk:** It keeps the ledger; you send the statement to the client yourself — money matters, don't be sloppy.

</details>

<a id="skill-060"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>060. Installment payment schedules</b> —— Big-ticket one-shot quotes scare clients; split installments and you fear not collecting.</summary>

Big-ticket one-shot quotes scare clients; split installments and you fear not collecting.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse the total and delivery milestones; have it split into "3-3-3-1" or "5-3-2" plans.

2. Tell it: "Tie payments to milestones — give me a schedule both sides sign off on."

3. Remind clients to pay before each delivery — money first, goods after, never reverse.

**Real talk:** It builds the schedule; you hold the "money first, goods after" line yourself — don't go soft.

</details>

---

## 📈 Finance & Investing

> Earnings reports, holdings, FX, insurance: see your money clearly first

<a id="skill-061"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>061. Earnings calls in three minutes</b> —— An hour of rapid English, and all you remember is the CEO…</summary>

Earnings calls run an hour in rapid-fire English, and all you remember is the CEO saying "strong momentum."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Drop the transcript into Muse: "Three things: did they beat expectations, did they raise guidance, what's bugging analysts most."

2. Add: "Versus last quarter, where did management's tone shift? Make it a table."

3. Save the takeaway to memory, and have it compare your holdings side by side at quarter-end.

**Real talk:** No brokerage API, so it can't see your holdings — you report your tickers and cost basis yourself. It handles the interpretation only.

</details>

<a id="skill-062"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>062. Ex-dividend date reminders</b> —— The price auto-adjusts, your statement flashes red, and you think…</summary>

The price auto-adjusts on the ex-dividend date, your statement suddenly flashes red, and you're convinced you lost money — heart attack.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your tickers and share counts; it looks up each ex-dividend date and dividend amount and logs them in your calendar.

2. Tell it: "Remind me the evening before the ex-dividend date, then once more before the open."

3. Screenshot the dividend deposit, have it archive it, and export the full year's dividend record at tax time.

**Real talk:** It can't see your brokerage holdings — tickers and share counts come from you. You confirm each deposit yourself; it just keeps the calendar.

</details>

<a id="skill-063"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>063. Friday portfolio auto-review</b> —— A week of ups and downs, and whether you're up or down is pure…</summary>

A week of ups and downs, and whether you're up or down — or which stock is dragging — is pure vibes.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Every Friday, send Muse your holdings screenshot: "Rank by weekly return — tell me who carried the gains and who's dragging."

2. Have it compare with last week's review: "Anything moving over 5% — find out what happened this week."

3. Set a recurring task so it asks for your data every Friday night. Reviews never skip a beat.

**Real talk:** No brokerage API, so you export the data manually each week. It interprets and compares — it never makes trades for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-064"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>064. Convert foreign spending to RMB in one shot</b> —— Swiping abroad felt great, but the statement is all foreign…</summary>

Swiping abroad felt great; back home the statement is all foreign currency, and you can't tell what the trip actually cost.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your statement screenshots: "Look up each transaction's date for that day's FX rate, convert everything to RMB, and table it."

2. Have it subtotal by category: "Food, transport, lodging — how much each? Make a table."

3. Save the table as a file; reuse the template on your next trip abroad.

**Real talk:** No bank API — screenshots or exports from you. Rates come from public data; for big conversions, go by your bank's actual quote.

</details>

<a id="skill-065"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>065. Insurance bills auto-filed</b> —— Renewal emails rot in your inbox until a failed payment kills…</summary>

Insurance renewal emails rot in your inbox until a failed payment kills your policy and you finally notice.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Have Muse search Gmail for insurance renewal notices: "List every premium due in the next three months — amounts and dates."

2. Tell it: "Remind me seven days before each due date, and flag which card gets charged."

3. Forward payment confirmations to it for archiving — a full year of payments, crystal clear.

**Real talk:** Gmail integration works fine for searching. But the actual payment happens in your insurer's or bank's app — Muse handles reminders and filing only.

</details>

<a id="skill-066"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>066. Credit card due-date manager</b> —— Too many cards to remember which is due when; one late payment…</summary>

Too many cards to remember which is due when; one late payment means fees plus a stain on your credit report. Expensive.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse each card's statement date, due date, and limit; it builds a payment calendar.

2. Tell it: "Remind me three days before each due date, and tell me roughly what this bill is."

3. Decide in advance which cards to pay in installments vs. in full, set the strategy with it, then execute on schedule.

**Real talk:** No bank API — you read the amounts in your banking app and report them. Muse keeps the calendar; your finger hits pay.

</details>

<a id="skill-067"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>067. DCA discipline enforcer</b> —— The auto-invest plan looked beautiful — until a dip made you pause…</summary>

The auto-invest plan looked beautiful — until a dip made you pause contributions, then the rebound made you kick yourself.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your plan: which fund, what day each month, how much — it stores it in long-term memory.

2. Have it remind you on debit day: "Time to invest today — tell me when it's done."

3. After three months, have it audit you: "How many times did I skip, and what was the market doing each time? List them."

**Real talk:** It can't touch your money — you confirm each debit in your bank or fund app. It just keeps you honest.

</details>

<a id="skill-068"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>068. A 50-page research report in one page</b> —— Bank research runs fifty pages in English; you're asleep by page…</summary>

Investment-bank research reports run fifty pages in English; you're asleep by page three and miss every key point.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the PDF to Muse: "One-page summary: core thesis, target-price logic, three biggest risks."

2. Follow up: "Split the evidence into facts vs. analyst assumptions — list them separately."

3. Archive the takeaway; when the same analyst publishes again, ask: "Did he flip this time?"

**Real talk:** It can do all three steps itself — you just send the PDF. But the buy/sell call is yours; it makes you understand, not rich.

</details>

<a id="skill-069"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>069. Bill screenshots auto-categorized</b> —— You've installed and deleted every expense app; manual entry is…</summary>

You've installed and deleted every expense app; manual entry is torture, and month-end money mysteries remain.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Batch-send Muse your payment screenshots: "Sort into dining, transport, shopping, entertainment — make a table."

2. Have it flag anomalies: "List any single charge over 500 separately — impulse buy?"

3. At month-end, have it produce a monthly report: "Which category overspent, and how should I budget next month?"

**Real talk:** WeChat and Alipay offer no API — you screenshot or export. It categorizes and crunches; the spending brakes are yours.

</details>

<a id="skill-070"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>070. Shareholder meeting minutes in minutes</b> —— Voting emails arrive yearly and get ignored yearly; dividend plans…</summary>

Shareholder voting emails arrive yearly and get ignored yearly; dividend plans pass while you weren't looking.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the proposal documents to Muse: "One sentence per proposal — what does voting yes mean for me?"

2. Dig into the executive pay one: "Anything fishy in there?"

3. Have it record your voting intentions; next year just ask, "How did I vote on this company last time?"

**Real talk:** It reads and explains steps 1–2 for you. But your vote is your call — and you cast it in your brokerage app; it can't vote for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-071"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>071. Valuation check: PE/PB/PEG</b> —— You like a stock; cheap or expensive is pure gut feel — then it traps…</summary>

You like a stock; cheap or expensive is pure gut feel — and then it traps you.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the ticker to Muse; it pulls PE, PB, PEG, and the industry averages.

2. Tell it: "Versus peers, which of the three looks expensive and which looks fine — table it."

3. Don't chase the expensive ones — have it set a "remind me when it's back in a reasonable range" alert, and wait patiently.

**Real talk:** It fetches the data; the buy/sell decision is yours. It doesn't place orders.

</details>

<a id="skill-072"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>072. How much tax gets taken from your dividend</b> —— Your dividend lands smaller than the announcement — and you…</summary>

Your dividend lands smaller than the announcement — and you think the brokerage shorted you.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your holding period and the dividend plan; it works out "the tax rate for my holding period."

2. Tell it: "On my position, how much actually lands in my account this time?"

3. Next time a juicy dividend is announced, have it compute after-tax yield before you get excited — don't let the headline number fool you.

**Real talk:** It does the tax math, but withholding is automatic — questions go to your brokerage's support.

</details>

<a id="skill-073"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>073. FX timing reminders</b> —— Tuition or a trip abroad — you convert on pure luck, and the rate drops…</summary>

Tuition or a trip abroad — you convert on pure luck, and the rate drops the moment you're done.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse how much you need and in what currency; set a "target rate in my head."

2. Tell it: "Ping me when the rate hits that number, with a quick read on the recent trend."

3. Don't go all in at once — have it build a "convert in three batches" plan.

**Real talk:** It's a reference only — the actual conversion happens in your banking app, by your hand. It can't move your money.

</details>

<a id="skill-074"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>074. Stock Connect hidden FX cost</b> —— Trades through Stock Connect look profitable — until FX costs eat…</summary>

Your trades through Stock Connect (the Hong Kong–mainland trading link) look profitable — until FX costs eat half the gain.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your Stock Connect settlement slips; it works out the true cost: "FX plus fees."

2. Tell it: "Versus converting to HKD and buying directly, which channel costs less?"

3. Before your next order, have it compute: "FX included, how far must the price rise to break even?"

**Real talk:** It does the cost math, but orders and conversions happen in your brokerage app — by you.

</details>

<a id="skill-075"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>075. The four-piece insurance setup</b> —— Agents push dividend policies daily, and you have no idea which…</summary>

Agents push dividend-paying policies daily, and you have no idea which coverage you're actually missing.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your age, family situation, and existing policies; it draws your "coverage gap map."

2. Tell it: "Rank the four pieces — accident, medical, critical illness, life — by priority for me."

3. When an agent pitches a product, send Muse the terms: is it filling a gap you actually have?

**Real talk:** It organizes the plan, but you sign the policy yourself — the health disclosure is on you.

</details>

<a id="skill-076"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>076. Medical insurance: three things to compare</b> —— Dozens of pages of terms — renewal, deductibles, reimbursement…</summary>

Medical insurance terms run dozens of pages — renewal, deductibles, reimbursement ratios make your head spin.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse two or three policies you're eyeing; it compares them on "renewal terms, deductible, coverage scope."

2. Tell it: "Flag the single worst clause in each one — I'll read those first."

3. Before deciding, have it draft 3 questions for the agent. If they can't answer, don't buy.

**Real talk:** It reads the fine print, but the health disclosure at signup is yours — fill it in honestly, no omissions.

</details>

<a id="skill-077"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>077. Buy accident insurance without the traps</b> —— Accident policies cost pennies a year — until you learn sudden…</summary>

Accident insurance costs pennies a year — until you learn sudden death isn't covered and disability payouts are scaled down.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your occupation and regular sports; it filters for products with "no occupation restrictions."

2. Tell it: "Compare these: is sudden death covered, how is disability paid, how many deductible days?"

3. A one-year term with enough coverage is all you need — don't get talked into long-term return-of-premium plans.

**Real talk:** It screens the products, but you make the claims call yourself and gather the paperwork.

</details>

<a id="skill-078"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>078. Car insurance renewal price check</b> —— The dealership calls, you say yes — and pay 800 more than buying…</summary>

Your car insurance renews, the dealership calls, you say yes — and pay 800 more than buying it yourself.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Photograph last year's policy for Muse; it splits out "must-haves vs. optionals."

2. Tell it: "Based on my car and claims history, give me this year's coverage plan."

3. Take the plan to two or three insurers for quotes. A bigger rebate isn't always better — coverage first.

**Real talk:** It designs the plan, but you buy and pay through official channels yourself — no sketchy back doors.

</details>

<a id="skill-079"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>079. The true rate on credit card installments</b> —— A "0.6% fee" sounds cheap — until the real APR turns out near…</summary>

A "0.6% fee" on bill installments sounds cheap — until the real APR turns out to be nearly 14%.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Screenshot the installment offer for Muse; it converts it to the "true annualized rate."

2. Tell it: "Versus paying it all now, how much extra interest am I paying?"

3. If the annualized rate tops 10%, skip installments — have it build you a payoff plan.

**Real talk:** It does the math, but the repayment happens in your banking app — by you, on time.

</details>

<a id="skill-080"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>080. Online-loan APR, decoded at a glance</b> —— "0.05% daily interest" sounds cheap — but the APR is really…</summary>

A lending app advertises "0.05% daily interest" — sounds cheap, but the APR is really 18%.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Screenshot the rate page for Muse; it converts it to an annualized rate.

2. Tell it: "Add in the fees and service charges — what's my real APR?"

3. Anything over 24%, close the tab — and have it find you cheaper ways to bridge the gap.

**Real talk:** It runs the numbers, but borrowing is your decision. Think twice before you tap.

</details>

<a id="skill-081"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>081. Should you prepay your mortgage?</b> —— Some spare cash on hand — prepay the mortgage or invest it? You've…</summary>

Some spare cash on hand — prepay the mortgage or invest it? You've lost sleep over it.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your balance, rate, and remaining years; it computes "how much interest prepaying 100k saves."

2. Tell it: "Compare with my current investment returns — which wins?"

3. Keep six months of living expenses; have it compute "how much to prepay without hurting daily life."

**Real talk:** It does the math, but the prepayment application goes to the bank by you — it can't sign for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-082"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>082. Housing-fund withdrawal reminders</b> —— Your housing fund naps in its account — and you had no idea renters…</summary>

Your housing fund naps in its account — and you had no idea renters can withdraw it.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your city and contribution status; it looks up "which withdrawal scenarios apply to you."

2. Tell it: "For a rental withdrawal, list the documents and the exact process."

3. Have it remind you yearly: did you use this year's quota? Don't let it go to waste.

**Real talk:** It looks up the policy, but you file the application in the government app yourself — it can't do that part.

</details>

<a id="skill-083"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>083. Tracking analyst opinions</b> —— The analyst was bullish last month, flipped this month — and you're…</summary>

The analyst was bullish last month, flipped this month — and you're still adding to the position on the old thesis.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the analysts and reports you follow; it builds an "opinion file."

2. Tell it: "When a new report lands, compare with the last one — did the thesis change, did the target move?"

3. Have it flag opinion reversals in red, so you don't make new decisions on stale logic.

**Real talk:** It watches the opinions; the trades are yours. Don't treat research reports as gospel.

</details>

<a id="skill-084"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>084. Listed-company announcements, speed-read</b> —— Your holding drops an announcement — a dozen-page PDF, and…</summary>

Your holding drops an announcement — a dozen-page PDF, and you give up after three lines.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the PDF to Muse: "Three sentences: what happened, and is it bullish or bearish?"

2. Tell it: "Has this happened before — and how did the stock react back then?"

3. Have it archive the important ones for an earnings-season review.

**Real talk:** It reads the announcement; the trading decision is yours. It doesn't place orders.

</details>

<a id="skill-085"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>085. Shareholder selloff alerts</b> —— Major shareholders are quietly selling while you're in the dark — you…</summary>

Major shareholders are quietly selling while you're in the dark — you only find out when the price drops.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your holdings; it watches for announcements like "shareholder reductions, executive resignations."

2. Tell it: "Tell me the moment a reduction is announced — with the ratio and the reason."

3. Don't panic at every reduction — have it analyze: full exit or routine trimming?

**Real talk:** It watches the filings; selling is your call. Don't trade on emotion.

</details>

<a id="skill-086"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>086. Reading buyback signals</b> —— The company announces a buyback, the stock falls anyway — and you…</summary>

The company announces a buyback, the stock falls anyway, and you can't read the signal at all.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the buyback announcement; it breaks down "amount, price cap, real intent."

2. Tell it: "Versus history: after this company's past buybacks, how long did the rally last?"

3. Don't take token buybacks seriously — have it sniff out "buyback theater."

**Real talk:** It interprets the signal, but real-money decisions are yours. Don't chase announcements.

</details>

<a id="skill-087"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>087. IPO subscription calendar</b> —— IPO subscriptions depend on friends' group-chat tips — by the time you…</summary>

IPO subscriptions depend on friends' group-chat tips — by the time you hear, you've already missed three.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your brokerage and market value; it computes "your subscription quota."

2. Tell it: "Every Monday, list this week's IPOs and which one deserves the max subscription."

3. Won the lottery? Have it remind you to fund the account — don't win and then default on payment.

**Real talk:** It manages the calendar, but you click subscribe in your brokerage app yourself — it can't place the order.

</details>

<a id="skill-088"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>088. Convertible-Bond IPO Alerts</b> —— Free to apply, but you keep forgetting — and missing the big wins.</summary>

Convertible-bond IPO subscriptions cost nothing, but you keep forgetting to apply and miss the profitable lottery wins.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your brokerage and have it round up "this week's subscribable convertible bonds" every week.

2. Ask it: "Rank by conversion premium — which one deserves the max subscription?"

3. Have it remind you once each at the two key moments: payment after winning, and selling after listing.

**Real talk:** It handles the reminders, but the subscribing and paying happen in your brokerage app — it can't place orders for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-089"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>089. Government-Bond Reverse Repo Cash Grab</b> —— Cash idles over the holiday while others collect days of free interest.</summary>

Idle cash sits in your account over the long holiday while others run reverse repos and pocket days of free interest.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Three days before a holiday, ask Muse: "Which repo term is the best deal this time?"

2. Tell it: "Based on ¥100,000, how much interest can I grab this time, net of fees?"

3. Have it remind you the morning before the holiday — miss it and you wait for the next one.

**Real talk:** It crunches the timing for you, but you place the order yourself in your brokerage app — it can't touch your money.

</details>

<a id="skill-090"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>090. Money-Market Fund Yield Showdown</b> —— Yu'e Bao (Alibaba's money-market fund) yields keep falling; too lazy to switch, losing hundreds yearly.</summary>

Yu'e Bao yields keep sinking, you're too lazy to switch, and it costs you hundreds a year.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse which money-market fund you're using and have it pull the "top-10 seven-day annualized yields" in the category.

2. Say: "On ¥100,000, how much more would #1 earn me in a year?"

3. Have it recheck the ranking quarterly — if yours drops out of the top ten, get a nudge to move.

**Real talk:** It does the comparing, but redeeming and buying happen in your app — it can't touch your money.

</details>

<a id="skill-091"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>091. Bank Deposit Rate Comparison</b> —— Locked into a 3-year CD at 1.5% while the small bank next door pays 2.2%.</summary>

Your money's locked in a 3-year CD at 1.5%, and you had no idea the small bank down the street pays 2.2%.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your deposit amount and term, and have it look up "the latest posted rates across banks."

2. Say: "On ¥200,000 for three years, how much extra does the top rate get me per year?"

3. For small banks, have it check "do they carry the deposit-insurance badge" — safety first.

**Real talk:** It compares the rates, but opening the account and depositing is on you — it can't go to the bank for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-092"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>092. US Brokerage Account Comparison</b> —— So many US brokers — commissions, transfers, tax forms make your head spin.</summary>

You want to buy US stocks, but the pile of brokerages, commissions, fund transfers, and tax forms makes your eyes glaze over.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your capital and trading frequency, and have it rank by "commissions + transfer costs."

2. Say: "Where's each one's catch: W-8BEN, account fees, withdrawal speed?"

3. Have it list everything you need for the application — prep it all at once, no back-and-forth.

**Real talk:** It does the comparing, but the video verification for account opening is you on camera — no impersonating that.

</details>

<a id="skill-093"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>093. HK IPO: The Real Cost Math</b> —— One winning HK IPO lot nets ¥2,000, until margin interest eats it all.</summary>

You win one HK IPO lot for a ¥2,000 gain, then margin interest and fees eat it all — and then some.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your brokerage's margin rate and have it calculate the "break-even pop for a margin subscription."

2. Say: "At this cost, which IPOs deserve margin, and which are fine with cash?"

3. On listing day, have it remind you to review: how much did this IPO really make?

**Real talk:** It does the cost math, but you confirm the margin subscription in your brokerage app yourself — the risk is yours.

</details>

<a id="skill-094"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>094. Fund Holdings Overlap Check</b> —— Five funds, zero real diversification — top holdings all the same baijiu stocks.</summary>

You hold 5 funds and think you're diversified — then you look and the top holdings are all the same baijiu stocks.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your fund codes and have it pull the "top-ten holdings" for a cross-comparison.

2. Say: "Flag anything with over 50% overlap — that's me paying double management fees."

3. Have it recommend one fund "least correlated with what I already hold" to round things out.

**Real talk:** It finds the overlap, but rebalancing happens in your app — it can't place orders.

</details>

<a id="skill-095"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>095. Fund Manager Change Alerts</b> —— Bought for the star manager — they left and you didn't even notice.</summary>

You bought it for the star manager, they got replaced without you knowing, and you only noticed after performance slid.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your holdings and have it build a "manager watch file" to keep an eye on.

2. Say: "Tell me the moment a change is announced — and how the new manager's track record looks."

3. If the new manager's style is totally different, have it assess: is this still worth holding?

**Real talk:** It watches the changes, but redeeming vs. holding is your call — don't let a famous name fool you.

</details>

<a id="skill-096"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>096. Index Fund Tracking Error</b> —— Both track CSI 300, but yours lags the index 2% a year and you had no idea.</summary>

They all track the CSI 300, but some funds trail the index by 2% a year — and you're still in the dark.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your index funds and have it check "one-year tracking error and expense ratios."

2. Say: "Which peer has the smallest error and lowest fee? Make me a table."

3. For the laggards, have it calculate: how much did the error cost you this year, and is switching worth it?

**Real talk:** It crunches the error, but the actual switch happens in your app — it can't move your money.

</details>

<a id="skill-097"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>097. Stacking Gold on a Budget</b> —— Gold's over ¥600 a gram; one big buy stings, and you're scared of the top.</summary>

Gold keeps climbing, over ¥600 a gram — buying a full lot in one go hurts, and you're scared of buying the peak.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse how much you can set aside monthly and have it design a "weekly accumulation plan."

2. Say: "Compare bank accumulation vs. jewelry-shop gram gold — which has lower fees?"

3. When gold dips hard, have it nudge you: time to add a bit? Don't chase rallies or panic-sell dips.

**Real talk:** It designs the plan, but you click buy in your bank app — it can't touch your money.

</details>

<a id="skill-098"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>098. QDII Premium Alerts</b> —— QDII funds (China's outbound investment scheme) premium hits 15% and you still buy in — you bleed before the NAV drops.</summary>

The QDII premium hits 15% and you still pile in — when the premium fades, you lose money before the NAV even falls.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse which QDII funds you hold and have it watch the "premium" daily.

2. Say: "Ping me the moment the premium passes 8% — don't let me buy the top."

3. For funds you want to buy, have it check: trading at a discount or a premium right now — worth waiting?

**Real talk:** It watches the premium, but you buy and sell QDII yourself in the app — don't chase the top.

</details>

<a id="skill-099"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>099. Income Tax Deduction Reminders</b> —— Mortgage interest, aging parents — all deductible, and you've never claimed any.</summary>

Mortgage interest, supporting elderly parents — all tax-deductible, and you haven't filed a single claim all year.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your situation: mortgage, kids, parents, continuing education — everything that applies.

2. Say: "Given all this, how much tax can I shave off a year? List it out."

3. Every December, have it remind you to confirm next year's special deductions — don't let them lapse.

**Real talk:** It calculates the savings, but the filing happens in your tax app — it can't do it for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-100"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>100. Year-End Bonus: Pick the Right Tax Method</b> —— Separate or combined taxation — pick wrong and overpay by thousands.</summary>

Tax your year-end bonus separately or fold it into annual income — pick wrong and you overpay by thousands.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your salary and bonus numbers and have it run both tax methods.

2. Say: "Which one saves tax, and by how much — give me a clear verdict."

3. Before payout, have it remind you to confirm the method with finance — no changing it after.

**Real talk:** It does the tax math, but confirming the method with finance is on you — don't miss the window.

</details>

<a id="skill-101"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>101. Private Pension Account</b> —— Private pensions shelter ¥12,000 a year from tax — and you never opened an account.</summary>

The private pension plan shelters ¥12,000 a year from taxes — and you haven't even opened an account.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your annual income and have it calculate "how much tax I'd save by opening and maxing out for a year."

2. Say: "Compare the sign-up perks across banks — pick the most convenient one."

3. Every December, have it remind you to hit the annual cap — the money can buy products inside the account.

**Real talk:** It calculates the savings, but opening the account and contributing happens in your bank app — it can't do it for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-102"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>102. Retake Your Risk Assessment</b> —— "Conservative" risk rating from 3 years ago, now you're all-in on ChiNext.</summary>

The risk assessment you took three years ago says "conservative" — now you're loaded up on ChiNext. They don't match at all.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your current holdings and have it check "does my risk level still match my assessment result?"

2. Say: "Based on my actual situation, draft a fresh set of reference answers for the assessment."

3. Have it remind you to retake it yearly — income and mindset change, so the assessment should too.

**Real talk:** It preps the questionnaire, but the real assessment happens in your brokerage app — fill it out honestly, don't just click through.

</details>

<a id="skill-103"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>103. Fund Dividends: Cash or Reinvest?</b> —— Dividends default to cash and you never switched — compounding wasted.</summary>

Fund dividends default to cash, you never changed it — and all that compounding goes to waste.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your funds' dividend settings and have it check whether each is "cash" or "reinvest."

2. Say: "Given how long I'll hold, how much more would switching to reinvest earn me in a year?"

3. Reinvest the money you won't need soon, keep cash for what you will — have it sort them into buckets.

**Real talk:** It does the math, but changing the dividend setting happens in your app — it can't change it for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-104"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>104. A Cooldown Period for Impulse Trades</b> —— See a limit-up screenshot in the group chat, chase it, bag-hold the top. Every time.</summary>

You see limit-up screenshots in the group chat, your hand shakes, you chase — and end up bag-holding the top, every single time.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Make a deal with Muse: "When I want to chase a hot stock, I tell you my reasoning first."

2. Have it play devil's advocate: list three reasons NOT to buy, and pour cold water on you.

3. Set a 24-hour cooldown — still want it after? Have it set you a "position cap."

**Real talk:** It pours the cold water, but the hand clicking buy is yours — keeping it in check is on you.

</details>

## 🛠️ Indie Hacking

> A one-person dev team: marketing, docs, user feedback

<a id="skill-105"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>105. Make Your README Hook in 3 Seconds</b> —— README reads like a manual, visitors bounce in 3 seconds…</summary>

Your README reads like an instruction manual. Visitors bounce in three seconds, and every star is an accident.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send your README to Muse: "Rewrite this from a first-time visitor's perspective — the first screen must say what problem this project solves."

2. Have it add a 30-second quickstart, the copy-paste-and-it-runs kind.

3. Finally, have it play critic: "Delete three technically-correct-but-useless sentences, and tell me which line makes a visitor close the page."

**Real talk:** It can do all three steps itself — you just copy and paste. But whether the project deserves to keep people is on your code. It can't write a good project for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-106"></a>

<details>
