<summary><b>106. Auto-Sort Issues + Reply Drafts</b> —— issues pile up by the dozen, bugs and questions and wishes…</summary>

Issues pile up by the dozen, bugs and questions and feature requests all mixed together, and you can't keep up with replies.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Export your issue list and send it to Muse: "Sort these into bugs, questions, and feature requests, ordered by urgency within each bucket."

2. Have it draft a reply for each one: "Polite but not wordy — for bugs, ask for reproduction steps first."

3. Skim them and paste directly as replies, spending the saved sorting time actually fixing bugs.

**Real talk:** It can't post comments — you paste the drafts by hand. And you export the list; it only handles the sorting and drafting.

</details>

<a id="skill-107"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>107. One-Click Changelog</b> —— every release means digging through commits to write…</summary>

Every release means digging through commit history to write the changelog, and forgetting features is routine.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Before a release, dump your commit list on Muse: "Group these into new features, fixes, and breaking changes, and write it as a changelog humans can read."

2. Have it translate jargon into plain English: "Don't write 'fix null pointer' — say exactly when the app crashes."

3. Save it as CHANGELOG.md and run the same process every release.

**Real talk:** You hand over the commit list by hand; saving the file and pushing is on you too.

</details>

<a id="skill-108"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>108. Auto-Stalk Your Competitors' Launches</b> —— a competitor quietly ships a feature, and by the time you notice…</summary>

A competitor quietly ships a new feature, and by the time you notice they've already harvested a wave of users.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse the competitor's site and changelog URLs and have it scan weekly: "If there's a feature update, tell me what they changed."

2. Follow up: "Do we have this feature? If not, roughly how many days to build it?"

3. Each month have it produce a comparison table: "How many feature gaps remain between us and them?"

**Real talk:** It handles all the stalking and comparing; but whether to follow and how to schedule it is your call — it can't set priorities for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-109"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>109. Auto-Summarize User Feedback</b> —— feedback scattered across comments, DMs, and email…</summary>

User feedback is scattered across comments, DMs, and email. Reading it all is the same as reading nothing, and redesigns run on gut feeling.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Gather all the feedback into one doc and send it to Muse: "Summarize the three most-mentioned problems."

2. Have it separate them: "Which are real pain points and which are one-off weird requests — list both."

3. Before a redesign, ask it: "Rank these by feedback volume."

**Real talk:** Gathering the feedback into a doc is on you — it can't reach into your comment backends. It handles all the summarizing and ranking, but you decide what gets fixed first.

</details>

<a id="skill-110"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>110. Translate English Tech Docs in Seconds</b> —— the open-source library you want has English-only docs…</summary>

The open-source library you want has English-only docs, and machine translation mangles the terminology — you end up more confused than before.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the doc link or content: "Translate this into Chinese, keeping code and proper nouns in English."

2. Have it add a plain-English explanation after each key concept: "Don't translate literally — explain what the thing actually does."

3. Save the translation as a doc so teammates with weak English can just read that one.

**Real talk:** It does all three steps itself — you just drop the link. But you need to eyeball the result as the technical person, because a mistranslated term will burn your whole team.

</details>

<a id="skill-111"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>111. Turn Code Comments into API Docs</b> —— the API is done but the docs aren't, and people keep asking…</summary>

The API is done but the docs aren't, and people keep asking you what to pass for that parameter.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send your API code to Muse: "Generate docs for each endpoint: URL, parameters, return values, and one runnable request example."

2. Have it mark required vs. optional: "Add a warning note on parameters that are easy to get wrong."

3. When an API changes, send it the new code: "Only update what changed."

**Real talk:** You send the code by hand; it does all the documenting. But if the API has real gotchas you'll have to flag them — it only reads the code literally.

</details>

<a id="skill-112"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>112. The 3-Part Landing Page Copy</b> —— your landing page reads like a feature list…</summary>

Your landing page reads like a feature list. Visitors finish it with no idea what it does for them.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send your product intro to Muse: "Write three lines of copy: the first on the pain, the second on the outcome, the third on 'start now.'"

2. Have it produce three versions: "One data-driven and rational, one story-driven and emotional, one short enough for a button."

3. After launch, send back the conversion data: "Which version converted best — match that tone next time."

**Real talk:** It handles all the copywriting; but step 3's launch and conversion data is on you — it can't see your analytics, and you call the final tone.

</details>

<a id="skill-113"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>113. Cheat Sheet for Pricing Pages</b> —— how to split tiers and how much to give away free…</summary>

How to split pricing tiers and how much to give away free — you're just copying competitors, and you still don't feel sure.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Screenshot three competitors' pricing pages and send them to Muse: "Make a comparison table: price per tier, core features, and limits."

2. Ask: "What does their free tier restrict, and where are the paywalls?"

3. Combine that with your costs and ask for advice: "If I price it like this, can my margins cover it?"

**Real talk:** The screenshots are yours to take; and your costs only you know — it can't compute your margins. The pricing call is yours.

</details>

<a id="skill-114"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>114. Cold-Launch Checklist for Indie Devs</b> —— three months building, launch day arrives…</summary>

Three months building, launch day arrives — and payments were never tested, analytics never wired, day one is all bugs.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. A week before launch, tell Muse: "I'm an indie dev shipping next week — give me a pre-launch checklist."

2. Walk it item by item: "Break payments, error monitoring, and backups into executable check items."

3. On launch day, have it verify each item: "Anything not passing — don't ship."

**Real talk:** It handles the whole checklist; but testing each item is yours — it can't get into your servers, and you click the launch button yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-115"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>115. The 5-Minute Indie Weekly Report</b> —— solo dev, pacing on vibes…</summary>

Solo dev, pacing on vibes — a week passes and you have no idea how much you actually moved forward.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Every Sunday, send Muse your week's commits and todos: "Summarize as a weekly report: what got done, where you're stuck, what's next."

2. Have it do the math: "How many days of real coding this week, and how many days got eaten by chores?"

3. After four weeks, have it review: "Is my pace speeding up or am I running in place this month?"

**Real talk:** You send the week's commits by hand; it only summarizes and reviews — what's next is still your call.

</details>

<a id="skill-116"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>116. Star Growth Post-Mortem</b> —— stars go up and you don't know why…</summary>

Stars go up and you don't know why they rose, stars go down and you don't know why they fell — pure astrology.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse a screenshot of your star growth curve: "Mark the fastest-growing days and help me recall what I did those days."

2. Ask about traffic sources: "Which post or feature brought these stars?"

3. Before your next move, ask it: "Based on history, which type of content grows stars fastest?"

**Real talk:** It can't see your GitHub traffic backend — you screenshot Insights. It just lines up the curve with what you did.

</details>

<a id="skill-117"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>117. SEO Keyword Matrix Content</b> —— six months blogging, zero traffic…</summary>

Six months blogging, zero traffic — SEO on pure luck, and you have no idea what users search for.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse a one-liner about your product: "List 20 long-tail keywords my target users would search, ranked by buying intent."

2. Take the top 5 and have it generate an outline for each: "Title carries the keyword, first 100 words must contain the answer."

3. Write to the outlines and publish, then after three months send back the search data and have it review which keyword types actually brought signups.

**Real talk:** It can't publish for you — you paste articles into your blog backend yourself, and the data you export from analytics is yours to hand over.

</details>

<a id="skill-118"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>118. Pick an Open-Source License in 3 Minutes</b> —— MIT, Apache, GPL…</summary>

MIT, Apache, GPL — the wall of text makes your head spin, and picking wrong is forever.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your situation: "Personal open-source project — I want everyone to use it freely, but I'm worried a big company will take it closed-source and commercial."

2. Have it compare MIT, Apache-2.0, and GPL: "Table format — commercial restrictions, whether modified code must be open-sourced, patent grants."

3. Once picked, have it generate the full LICENSE text plus a one-line README statement for you to copy into the repo.

**Real talk:** It's not a lawyer — before you commit, check the original text on the official site yourself. Don't take its word alone.

</details>

<a id="skill-119"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>119. Replying to App Store 1-Star Reviews</b> —— a 1-star review sits for three days…</summary>

A 1-star review sits in the app store for three days. Replying stiffly sounds like a bot, ignoring it kills conversions.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the review screenshot: "Write three reply drafts — empathy, explanation, and humor routes — 50 words max each."

2. Pick one, tweak it, copy it to the store backend as a reply, and log the bug the review mentions.

3. Each week, dump new reviews on it to categorize: "Which are real bugs and which are user error" — real bugs go into the backlog.

**Real talk:** It can't get into the store backend — the send button is yours to press. It only writes.

</details>

<a id="skill-120"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>120. Three Pricing Psychology Tricks</b> —— pricing by gut feeling…</summary>

Pricing by gut feeling — too low and you lose money, too high and nobody buys, and every price change is nerve-wracking.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your costs and competitor prices: "Give me three pricing plans using the anchoring effect, including one high anchor."

2. Have it design the display order: "Show the annual discount first, then monthly — make the middle tier shine."

3. After launch, give it the conversion data: "Which tier sold best, and should I add 99 endings?" and let it suggest tweaks.

**Real talk:** The final price is yours to change in the backend — it can't touch your payment system, and the data is yours to export.

</details>

<a id="skill-121"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>121. User Interview Question List</b> —— you sit down with a user and all you can ask is…</summary>

You sit down with a user and all you can ask is "so how do you like my product" — all small talk.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse what the product does: "Give me 10 user interview questions that dig into how users currently hack around this problem."

2. Have it add 3 follow-up scripts: "When a user says 'it's fine,' how do I pull out the truth?"

3. After the interview, send it the transcript: "Extract 5 verbatim-level pain points" and file them in the backlog.

**Real talk:** Booking people and sitting through interviews is on you — it can't chat with humans in your place. It only helps you prepare and debrief.

</details>

<a id="skill-122"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>122. New Feature Launch Announcement</b> —— two weeks on a new feature, the post gets 3 likes…</summary>

Two weeks on a new feature, the announcement gets 3 likes — users don't even know you shipped.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the feature description: "Write a 100-word launch announcement — lead with what the user gets, not the tech."

2. Have it adapt three versions: an X post, a group announcement, and a changelog entry, each with its own tone.

3. After posting, collect the comment questions: "Turn them into an FAQ" and paste it with the next release.

**Real talk:** You hit publish yourself — it can't log into platforms and post for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-123"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>123. Waitlist Landing Page</b> —— the product isn't built yet…</summary>

The product isn't built yet, and you want to validate whether anyone wants it — but you don't know where to start.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse the product in one line: "Write waitlist landing page copy — headline sells the outcome, subhead sells how, button says 'Get Early Access.'"

2. Have it design three hooks — early-bird pricing, lifetime membership, beta invite — and you pick one to feature.

3. After the page goes live, give it the traffic and signup data: "Does the headline need swapping?" and let it point the optimization direction.

**Real talk:** You build the page yourself — it only gives copy and ideas. It can't ship you a finished page.

</details>

<a id="skill-124"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>124. The One-Page Investor Sheet</b> —— an investor wants a one-pager…</summary>

An investor wants a one-pager, and you wrote five pages that still don't say what you do.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your product pitch: "Compress it into one page — pain point, solution, market, business model, team, three lines max per block."

2. Have it write a 30-second elevator version: "Memorizable in an elevator with an investor."

3. Test it on a friend, note what they didn't get, and hand that back for rewrites until a non-technical person understands.

**Real talk:** Don't fabricate the numbers — your fundraising data is yours to vouch for. It helps you say it; it can't back you up.

</details>

<a id="skill-125"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>125. Tech Stack Comparison Table</b> —— picking a framework, picking a database…</summary>

Picking a framework, picking a database — forums argue both sides, and the more you read the less you dare to start.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your scenario: "Solo dev, need to ship fast, expecting 10k users — Postgres or Mongo?"

2. Have it build a comparison table: "List learning curve, hosting costs, and future migration difficulty — not just features."

3. Once decided, have it sketch the minimal viable project's directory structure: "Just get it running first."

**Real talk:** It's giving you experience plus reasoning — before you commit, check current pricing and versions on the official sites yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-126"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>126. Human-Friendly Error Messages</b> —— a user hits an error and freezes…</summary>

A user hits an error and freezes — "Error 500" makes them close the page and leave.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Copy your product's 10 most common error messages to Muse: "Rewrite them in human language — each one says what happened and what the user can do now."

2. Have it write action-button copy for each: "Like 'Retry' or 'Contact Support' — not just 'OK.'"

3. After shipping, watch support volume: "Did these questions drop?" If yes, replace them all.

**Real talk:** You make the copy changes in code yourself — it can't touch your repo. And test it yourself after.

</details>

<a id="skill-127"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>127. Onboarding Copy</b> —— a new user signs up, clicks around…</summary>

A new user signs up, clicks around, and leaves — the core features never got opened once.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your core feature list: "Design a 3-step onboarding — each step one line saying what they get."

2. Have it write the button copy: "No 'Next' — use action words like 'Try It.'"

3. Give it the 7-day retention data: "Which step leaks the most?" and have it rewrite that step.

**Real talk:** You implement the onboarding flow in the product yourself — it only writes copy, and the analytics data is yours to export.

</details>

<a id="skill-128"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>128. The Welcome Email Series</b> —— a user subscribes to your email list…</summary>

A user subscribes to your email list, and the welcome email says nothing but "Subscription confirmed" — the first touchpoint wasted.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your product and audience: "Write 3 welcome emails — the first says who you are, the second delivers value, the third pitches the product."

2. Have it brainstorm 5 subject lines per email: "Optimize for open rate — no clickbait."

3. Two weeks after sending, give it the open-rate data: "Which one was worst?" and have it rewrite that one.

**Real talk:** You send the emails with your own email tool — it can't send. And the data is yours to export from the backend.

</details>

<a id="skill-129"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>129. Win Back Cancelling Users</b> —— A user hits cancel, the page just says "cancelled"…</summary>

A user hits cancel, the page just says "cancelled" — and they leave without you ever learning why.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse a screenshot of your cancellation flow: "Design a save popup. Asking why? Give 4 options plus a 'too expensive' one."

2. Have it write one save line per reason: "For 'too expensive', pop up an annual plan at 30% off. For 'can't figure it out', pop up a tutorial."

3. Feed it the monthly cancellation-reason stats: "Which reason is the most common?" Fix that one first.

**Real talk:** You have to build the popup and the discount in your product yourself — it can only give you the copy and the strategy. It can't ship anything.

</details>

<a id="skill-130"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>130. Turn Happy Users into Case Studies</b> —— A user loves your product but their testimonial is…</summary>

A user loves your product and you want to turn them into a case study, but all they replied was "works great."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the chat history to Muse: "Distill this into a case study structure: background, how they coped before, what changed after, numbers."

2. Have it write 5 follow-up questions — "the kind that pull out real numbers, like how many hours they saved."

3. Take the questions to the user, feed their answers back, and have it write a 300-word case study. Publish only after they sign off.

**Real talk:** The user has to personally approve it before you publish, and don't invent numbers for the case study — it can help you write it, not vouch for it.

</details>

<a id="skill-131"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>131. "Us vs. Competitor" Comparison Page</b> —— Users keep asking how you differ from XX, and…</summary>

Users keep asking "how are you different from XX" and you improvise the answer every time.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your feature list and the competitor's: "Build a comparison table — pick the dimensions where you win, and write 'on the roadmap' for the ones you lose."

2. Have it write a 50-word summary: "Don't trash the competitor, just say who you're right for."

3. Put the page out, collect new user questions, and "update monthly with anything the table doesn't cover."

**Real talk:** Verify the competitor's info yourself — don't let it invent their features. If it gets them wrong, you're the one who gets embarrassed.

</details>

<a id="skill-132"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>132. Free-Tier Hook Design</b> —— Tons of free users, under 1% convert — your free…</summary>

Tons of free users, under 1% converting to paid — your free tier is too generous.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your free and paid feature lists: "Find 3 things that 'work on free but feel cramped' and turn them into paid hooks."

2. Have it design the trigger timing: "e.g., on the 10th use, pop up: upgrade for unlimited."

3. After launch, watch conversion: "Which hook gets the most clicks?" Move the best one earlier.

**Real talk:** Feature flags and popups you build in code yourself — it can only give you the strategy. It can't touch your product.

</details>

<a id="skill-133"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>133. Open Source Sponsor Page Copy</b> —— Thousands of stars, sponsor button up for half a year…</summary>

Thousands of stars, sponsor button up for half a year — total sponsorships received: zero.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your project intro: "Write a sponsor page that says exactly where the money goes: servers, domain, your time."

2. Have it design three tiers: "$5 gets you on the thank-you list, $50 gets your issue fixed first."

3. Update it monthly: "Which issues sponsorships fixed this month," so sponsors see where the money went.

**Real talk:** Sponsorship money goes into your own account — it can't touch money, and you maintain the thank-you list yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-134"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>134. Browser Extension Store Listing</b> —— Extension is built, but the store description is bone-dry…</summary>

Your browser extension is done, but the store description is bone-dry and installs won't budge.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your extension's features: "Write a store description — first sentence says what problem it solves — plus captions for 3 screenshots."

2. Have it write 5 title options: "Keyword-rich, under 45 characters."

3. After launch, feed it the store's conversion data: "Lots of views, few installs" — and have it rewrite the first screen.

**Real talk:** You walk the review and submission process yourself — it can't click submit for you, and you have to take the screenshots yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-135"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>135. App Store Keywords</b> —— Your app only shows up when people search its exact name…</summary>

Your app only shows up when people search its exact name — search a feature word and you're past rank 50.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your app's features: "List 20 Chinese keywords users would search, grouped by search intent."

2. Have it write the subtitle and keyword field: "Pack the core terms into 100 characters, no stuffing."

3. Swap in a new set of keywords each release: "Track the ranking changes" and have it review which set worked.

**Real talk:** You type the keywords into the store backend yourself — it can't get in, and you check the rankings yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-136"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>136. Pricing Experiment Review</b> —— You changed the price once, sales moved, but you can't…</summary>

You changed the price once, sales moved, but you can't say whether it was the price or just luck.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse the before/after data: "List conversion rate and average order value by week, excluding the big-sale weeks."

2. Have it do the math: "Price up 20% — how much conversion can I lose before it hurts?" It gives you a break-even line.

3. Have it write a conclusion template: "Before the next price change, fill in: hypothesis, what changes, what metrics to watch, how many weeks to run."

**Real talk:** You export the data from your own backend — it can't see your payment dashboard, and the final call is yours.

</details>

<a id="skill-137"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>137. Churned-User Win-Back Survey</b> —— Users slip away silently, you want to ask why, but…</summary>

Users slip away silently. You want to ask why, but your survey gets under 5% response.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your churned-user profile: "Write a survey invite email with a subject line like '3 minutes, and I'll gift you a free month of membership.'"

2. Have it design 5 questions: "All multiple choice, open-ended only at the end, done in under 2 minutes."

3. Feed it the responses when they're in: "Summarize the top 3 churn reasons" — and schedule the fixes.

**Real talk:** You send the email yourself — it can't send anything, and you have to grant the free memberships in your backend yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-138"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>138. Backlog Prioritization</b> —— 50 requests logged, every one "urgent" — what gets built…</summary>

50 feature requests logged, every one "urgent" — what gets built depends on who shouts loudest.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse the request list: "Score them RICE-style: reach, impact, confidence, effort."

2. Have it flag the 5 "high value, low cost" ones: "Build these 5 this week."

3. Re-rank monthly: "Remove what's shipped, add what users just asked for" — keep the backlog fresh.

**Real talk:** You estimate the effort column yourself — it can't size your codebase. The score is just a reference; you're the one who decides.

</details>

<a id="skill-139"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>139. Tech Debt Audit</b> —— The code gets scarier to touch with every commit — changing a button…</summary>

The code gets scarier to touch with every commit — changing a button feels like it could break payments. You're drowning in debt.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your stack: "Give me a tech-debt audit template, ranked P0 to P3 by 'what happens if this blows up.'"

2. Tell it each landmine you know: "It ranks and sorts them — the P0s go into next week's plan."

3. Each fix, have it write a 50-word note: "What got fixed, how to avoid it next time" — they accumulate into team standards.

**Real talk:** It can't see your code — the whole audit runs on what you tell it. Hide something and it can't find it either.

</details>

<a id="skill-140"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>140. Generate a Contributor Guide</b> —— Someone wants to open a PR, but the README has no…</summary>

Someone wants to open a PR, but the README has no contribution guidelines — every newcomer asks the same questions.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your project details: "Write a CONTRIBUTING.md covering setup, branch naming, PR template, code style."

2. Have it add a "first contribution" section: "From fork to PR, every step with the exact commands."

3. Wherever newcomers get stuck following the guide, note it down: "Hand it back to fix" — the guide gets smoother each round.

**Real talk:** You verify the setup steps by running them yourself — it can't run your code, and if it writes a wrong step, newcomers get stuck.

</details>

<a id="skill-141"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>141. Security Vulnerability Response</b> —— A vulnerability report lands in your inbox, your stomach…</summary>

A vulnerability report lands in your inbox, your stomach drops — patch the hole first, or reply to the email first?

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the vulnerability description (sanitized): "Assess the blast radius, give me a P0 / P1 / P2 rating."

2. Have it write a response checklist: "Reproduce, patch, release, publish the advisory, thank the reporter — skip nothing."

3. Have it draft the security advisory: "State the affected versions and how to upgrade — leave out the exploit details."

**Real talk:** Don't paste the raw vulnerability details — sanitize first. You patch and release yourself; it can't touch your servers.

</details>

<a id="skill-142"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>142. Outage Announcement Templates</b> —— The service is down, users are spamming the group chat…</summary>

The service is down, users are spamming the group chat, and you're still thinking about how to start the first announcement.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your service type: "Write three tiers of outage templates: partial failure, total outage, data at risk."

2. Have it set the update cadence: "Update every 30 minutes, even if it's just 'still fixing it.'"

3. After recovery, have it write the post-mortem: "Explain the cause and what changed — don't blame the cloud provider."

**Real talk:** You post the announcements to each platform yourself — it can't post anything; and you're the one who fixes the outage.

</details>

<a id="skill-143"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>143. Price Increase Letter</b> —— You need to raise prices, users will be furious — the letter's…</summary>

You need to raise prices and users will be furious — the letter's gone through eight drafts and you still can't hit send.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your reasons for the increase: "Write a price-increase notice — lead with the new value added, then the price change."

2. Have it add a grandfather clause: "e.g., existing users keep the old price for 6 months" — pick one to use.

3. Before sending, run it past 5 friendly customers: "Mark the lines that sting," then have it rewrite until nothing stings.

**Real talk:** You change the price in your backend yourself — it can't do that; you send the notice yourself, it can only help you write it.

</details>

<a id="skill-144"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>144. Trial Expiry Win-Back Emails</b> —— Trial users expire and quietly vanish — your reminder email…</summary>

Trial users expire and quietly vanish — your reminder email reads like a debt collector, and conversions are miserable.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your trial flow: "Design three emails: 3 days before expiry, on the day, 3 days after."

2. Have each one include the user's trial data: "You used it 37 times in 14 days — one more step and it pays for itself."

3. Feed it the conversion data: "Which email got opened the most?" Then reuse that winner's subject line on the others.

**Real talk:** You send the emails with your own tooling — it can't send anything; and you export the user data from your backend yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-145"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>145. Product Naming Brainstorm</b> —— 20 names dreamed up, every one taken at the trademark office…</summary>

20 names dreamed up, every one taken at the trademark office — each round makes it worse.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse what the product does and who it's for: "Come up with 30 names in three buckets: literal, invented words, evocative."

2. Have it filter: "Cut the hard-to-read, hard-to-spell, ambiguous ones" — down to 10.

3. You check trademarks and domains yourself: "Send back the registrable ones," and have it rank them by memorability.

**Real talk:** You check trademarks and domains through official channels yourself — it can't check live registration status. Don't believe it when it says "this one's available."

</details>

<a id="skill-146"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>146. Picking a Domain Extension</b> —— The .com is taken, .io runs hundreds a year, .ai costs…</summary>

The .com is taken, .io runs hundreds a year, .ai costs even more — is it worth it?

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your product type and budget: "Compare .com, .io, .ai, .co — price, user trust, SEO impact."

2. Have it recommend: "Consumer products grab .com first, dev tools can live with .io, AI products can consider .ai."

3. Once decided, you place the order with a registrar yourself: "Register the common misspellings too, against phishing."

**Real talk:** It can only give you approximate prices — check the live price at your registrar before buying. You click the register button yourself too.

</details>

<a id="skill-147"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>147. Blog Topic Calendar</b> —— You want the blog to drive traffic, but it dies after three posts…</summary>

You want the blog to drive traffic, but it dies after three posts — topics come purely from inspiration.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your product and target users: "Plan 12 blog topics across four buckets — tutorials, cases, opinions, comparisons — 3 per month."

2. Have it write a one-line outline per topic: "Keyword in the title, first 3 lines must hook."

3. Each month, feed it the traffic data: "Which posts drove signups?" Tilt next month's topics toward that bucket.

**Real talk:** You write the posts yourself, and you hit publish yourself — it can't keep your blog updated for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-148"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>148. First Appearance in a Dev Community</b> —— You've been lurking for six months, wanting to get…</summary>

You want to get known in dev communities, but you've been lurking for six months and can't figure out what to post first.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse about your project: "Write an intro post — say what problem you solved, attach a GIF, don't beg for stars on arrival."

2. Have it list 5 communities that fit you: "V2EX, Juejin, Indie Hackers, ranked by vibe."

3. After posting, collect the comment-section questions: "Turn them into an FAQ," so your next post can just link it.

**Real talk:** You post yourself — it can't post; and you reply to the community yourself. Don't flood it with AI-generated spam.

</details>

<a id="skill-149"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>149. Open Source Launch Plan</b> —— Three months polishing the project, launch day arrives — one post…</summary>

Three months polishing the project, launch day arrives — one post, and stars went up by 2.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your project intro: "Write a launch-week plan: teaser Monday, Show HN Wednesday, Juejin Chinese edition Friday."

2. Have it write a version per platform: "The HN version talks implementation, the Juejin version talks what problem it solves."

3. After launch, feed it each platform's numbers: "Where did the stars come from?" Next launch, follow that rhythm.

**Real talk:** You post to each platform yourself — it can't post; you pick the posting times yourself, it can't stay up for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-150"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>150. Paywall Copy</b> —— The free-to-paid popup reads like a toll booth — users take one…</summary>

The free-to-paid popup reads like a toll booth — users take one look and close it.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your paid features: "Write a paywall — the headline says what the user is missing, don't write 'Upgrade to Pro.'"

2. Have it design the price display: "Annual up front, do the math — 'less than 1 yuan a day' style."

3. Feed it the popup's conversion rate: "Lots of lookers, few buyers" — have it rewrite the headline from a new angle.

**Real talk:** You build the popup in your product yourself — it can only give you copy; and you pull the conversion data from your analytics yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-151"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>151. Referral Program</b> —— The product is good, but users finish and walk away — they…</summary>

The product is good, but users finish and walk away — they never think of recommending it.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse your product and pricing: "Design a referral reward — both sides get a free month, none of that complicated points stuff."

2. Have it write the referral page copy and share script: "One line explains it: refer a friend and you both skip a month's bill."

3. After launch, watch the data: "Do referred users stick around better?" If not, have it try a different reward.

**Real talk:** You grant the rewards in your backend yourself — it can't hand out memberships; and you watch for reward farmers yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-152"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>152. Sunsetting a Feature, Gracefully</b> —— A feature nobody uses needs to go, but you're afraid…</summary>

A feature nobody uses needs to go, but you're afraid the old users will revolt — you've been putting it off for six months.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse the feature and its usage data: "Write a sunset announcement — say why it's going, what happens for old users, how to export their data."

2. Have it set the timeline: "Notify 30 days ahead, remind once more 7 days out" — you execute accordingly.

3. After the sunset, feed it the feedback: "Which sentence got people the angriest?" Dodge that trap next time.

**Real talk:** You post the announcement yourself — it can't post; and you build the data export yourself, it can only help with the copy.

</details>

---

## 💼 Work & Productivity

> Meetings, todos, emails, docs — fewer overtime nights

<a id="skill-153"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>153. Instant Alerts for Important Email</b> —— dozens of emails a day, and your client's payment confirmation is buried in a pile of ads — by the time you see it, half a day…</summary>

Dozens of emails a day, and your client's payment confirmation is buried in a pile of ads — by the time you see it, half a day is gone.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "Watch my Gmail. The moment an email from any of these people lands, tell me right away" — give it the sender list.

2. Add a rule: if the subject has words like contract, payment, invoice, or urgent, treat it as important and flag it too.

3. Everything else can wait — have it roll it all into one digest and send it to you at 9 PM.

**Real talk:** The sender list is on you to provide. It watches and nudges — but whatever's inside the email is still your job to handle.

</details>

<a id="skill-154"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>154. Pre-Meeting Briefs</b> —— you only realize five minutes before a meeting that you know nothing about the attendees or where things left off — then you're…</summary>

You only realize before a meeting that you know nothing about the attendees or where things left off — then you're walking in and winging it.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "For every meeting on my calendar, send me a pre-meeting brief 30 minutes ahead."

2. Lock the brief to three things: who the attendees are, where the last conversation left off, and what I might need to decide this time.

3. It'll dig through your calendar and emails for clues. After the first brief, tell it what was missing and it'll fill it in next time.

**Real talk:** It handles the whole brief — but you're the one who has to say what's missing. It can't guess what you actually care about in a meeting.

</details>

<a id="skill-155"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>155. Nagging for Overdue To-Dos</b> —— writing a to-do list is the same as not writing one: nobody nudges you when things slip, and you…</summary>

Writing a to-do list is the same as not writing one: nobody nudges you when things slip, and you forget the whole thing yourself.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "List all my Google Tasks to-dos, each with its deadline."

2. Then set a hard rule: "Every morning at 9, remind me of what's due today and what's already overdue — and don't be gentle."

3. When you finish something, just say "done with this" and it crosses it off, no more nagging.

**Real talk:** Listing and nagging are its job — even the harsh tone is still just a reminder. The actual work is yours: finish it, tell it, and only then does it count.

</details>

<a id="skill-156"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>156. Daily Reports</b> —— the worst part of the day is the end-of-day report: you stare at the screen wondering what you even did today, and…</summary>

The worst part of the day is the end-of-day report: you stare at the screen wondering what you even did today, and all you can squeeze out is "in progress, following up."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "Remind me at 5:30 PM every day to sum up today in three sentences."

2. You just reply with three sentences — it expands them into a proper report: what you did, where you're stuck, what's next tomorrow.

3. After a week, it stitches them into a weekly report draft. Copy, paste, submit — just fix the numbers yourself.

**Real talk:** Those three sentences are on you — it can't invent what your day looked like. It only expands and stitches. Give the numbers a look before you hit send.

</details>

<a id="skill-157"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>157. Shared Doc Change Alerts</b> —— someone quietly edited the shared project doc, nobody said a word, and you spent a week grinding on…</summary>

Someone quietly edited the shared project doc, nobody said a word, and you spent a week grinding away on the old version.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. The first time, tell Muse: "Memorize this version of the doc" — send it the full text or the link so it can archive it.

2. Every time you get a new version, throw it at it and say: "Compare with the last version, list every change."

3. It flags additions, deletions, and rewrites — and for the important ones, it'll even tell you to go confirm with whoever changed it.

**Real talk:** Muse can't get real-time change notifications — spotting that "there's a new version" is on you. You send the new version, it finds every difference in seconds.

</details>

<a id="skill-158"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>158. Auto-Drafted Weekly Reports</b> —— Friday afternoon you tear your hair out over the weekly report: the whole week's work is one blur, and what…</summary>

Friday afternoon you tear your hair out over the weekly report: the whole week's work is one blur, and what comes out reads like a diary of nothing.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "Before I leave every day, remind me to spend one minute saying three things: what I did, what moved forward, what's stuck."

2. It saves them daily, and Friday it auto-assembles the draft: done this week, in progress, risks — three sections.

3. You just fix the numbers and tone — it remembers all the facts and won't make things up.

**Real talk:** That one minute a day is on you — it can't recall your day for you. The draft can be perfect, but the numbers and the send button are still yours.

</details>

<a id="skill-159"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>159. Meeting Minutes</b> —— after every meeting there's a pile of recordings nobody listens to — two weeks later people argue "that's not…</summary>

After every meeting there's a pile of recordings nobody listens to — two weeks later people argue "that's not what was said," and there's no proof either way.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. After a meeting, send it the transcript: "Turn this into minutes: decisions, to-dos (with owner and deadline), open items."

2. Have it write the to-dos straight into your Google Tasks with deadlines, so they nag you automatically.

3. Before the next meeting, have it review last time's open items — walk in already knowing the status.

**Real talk:** Your meeting software doesn't talk to it, so the transcript is on you to export — from the meeting app or a recording-to-text — and then it handles the organizing and filing.

</details>

<a id="skill-160"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>160. Targeted Resume Rewrites</b> —— one resume blasted to 50 companies, and it's crickets everywhere — HR scans for six seconds, sees no keywords…</summary>

One resume blasted to 50 companies, and it's crickets everywhere — HR scans for six seconds, sees no keywords, and tosses it.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the job description plus your resume: "Rewrite my resume for this JD — match its keywords."

2. Have it flag JD words missing from your resume. Mark gaps honestly as "can add" — don't let it fabricate experience.

3. For every new company, toss it the new JD — ten minutes, one tailored resume.

**Real talk:** Steps 1 and 3 need you to supply the materials. It rewrites and aligns keywords — but whether you pass the interview is on your actual skills.

</details>

<a id="skill-161"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>161. Interview Question Prep</b> —— they ask "tell me about your hardest project" and your mind goes blank — the perfect answer only hits…</summary>

They ask "tell me about your hardest project" and your mind goes blank — the perfect answer only hits you on the way home.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the JD: "Pretend you're this company's interviewer. Give me the 10 most likely questions based on this JD."

2. Answer by voice or text — it critiques like an interviewer: where you're too vague, where you're missing numbers.

3. For your two weakest questions, have it drill you for three rounds until the answers roll off your tongue.

**Real talk:** Questions and critique are its job — but in step 2 the practicing is yours. On interview day it can't answer for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-162"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>162. English Email Polish</b> —— writing emails to foreigners, you re-read three times before daring to hit send — and even then you…</summary>

Writing emails to foreigners, you re-read three times before daring to hit send — and even then you worry the tone is off and the wording is embarrassing.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Paste your English draft to Muse: "Polish this into natural business English — don't change my meaning."

2. Have it show which lines changed and why — next time you'll know it yourself.

3. Tell it the stakes upfront: formal for clients, casual for colleagues.

**Real talk:** Polishing is its job — but the formal-vs-casual call is yours. Read it once yourself before sending — if it goes out wrong, that's not on it.

</details>

<a id="skill-163"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>163. Timezone Conversion</b> —— a US client says "Thursday at 3 PM" and you spend forever converting, still scared you messed up…</summary>

A US client says "Thursday at 3 PM" and you spend forever converting, still scared you messed up daylight saving.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "Whenever someone proposes a time across timezones, convert it to Beijing time for me."

2. Give it the other person's city — "they're in New York," say — and it'll handle daylight saving automatically.

3. Once confirmed, have it drop it into Google Calendar with both cities' times in the title — clear at a glance.

**Real talk:** Conversion is its job — but step 2 needs you to say which city. It can't guess who you're meeting, and if the meeting lands wrong, that's not on it.

</details>

<a id="skill-164"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>164. Capturing Stray Ideas</b> —— good ideas strike in the shower or on a walk — you're sure you'll remember, and by night they're…</summary>

Good ideas strike in the shower or on a walk — you're sure you'll remember, and by night they're all gone. Not one survives.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "You're my idea inbox. Whenever I drop a line on you, save it first — no judging."

2. Send ideas whenever they hit — one sentence is enough. It auto-tags and files them: work, money, life, inspiration.

3. Every Sunday, have it round them up: "List this week's ideas and pick the three most worth thinking about — expand on those."

**Real talk:** Step 2 is on you — it can't conjure your inspiration. It just stores and sorts. Which one is worth doing is still your call.

</details>

<a id="skill-165"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>165. Expense Receipt Organizing</b> —— invoices pile up in a drawer, and month-end you're pasting receipts till midnight — only for finance to…</summary>

Invoices pile up in a drawer, and month-end you're pasting receipts till midnight — only for finance to send them back.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Photograph your invoices one by one and send them to Muse: "Pull out the amount, date, and vendor — organize into a table."

2. Have it categorize them your company's way: "Rides, meals, hotels separate — flag anything over budget in red."

3. Print, paste, and "snap a photo to archive" — next time finance asks, you just pull up the record.

**Real talk:** The actual expense claim you file yourself in the system — it can't get into the company OA, and the originals are yours to keep safe.

</details>

<a id="skill-166"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>166. Business Trip Expense Filing</b> —— back from a trip and filing expenses: itinerary in one app, hotel bill in another, ride receipts in…</summary>

Back from a trip and filing expenses: itinerary in one app, hotel bill in another, ride receipts in a third — collecting everything takes half a day.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Before the trip, tell Muse your itinerary: "Make a checklist — flights, hotel, rides, meal allowance, and note which ones need receipts."

2. Mid-trip, forward it your e-invoices as they come: "It'll file them by day, so a missing day is obvious at a glance."

3. Back home, have it total everything into an expense sheet — "you copy it into the OA system and submit."

**Real talk:** You click submit in the OA yourself — it can't get into the company system. Paper receipts you paste yourself, too.

</details>

<a id="skill-167"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>167. Performance Review Deck Outline</b> —— your promotion review deck is 30 pages, all of it a stream of consciousness — the panel listens…</summary>

Your promotion review deck is 30 pages, all of it a stream of consciousness — the panel listens and remembers nothing about you.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. List your year's projects for Muse: "Boil them into 5 highlights — each one: the goal, what you did, the results with numbers."

2. Have it build the deck outline: "First slide states the conclusion: why I deserve this promotion. Every slide after is one piece of evidence."

3. Do a dry run with a colleague — "note where they yawn" — and have it rewrite those slides.

**Real talk:** Don't fabricate numbers. You build the deck yourself — it only gives the outline and copy. And when it's showtime, you're the one on stage.

</details>

<a id="skill-168"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>168. Setting Quarterly OKRs</b> —— your OKRs read like KPIs, all "complete XX" — come quarter-end review you realize it's all…</summary>

Your OKRs read like KPIs, all "complete XX" — come quarter-end review you realize it's all been self-congratulatory.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your responsibilities: "Write 3 objectives as 'goal (qualitative) + 3 measurable key results.'"

2. Have it audit: "Flag any unmeasurable words in the KRs — 'strive,' 'push forward' — and fix them."

3. At month-end, send it your progress: "Which KR is behind?" — and have it break the next actions down.

**Real talk:** You align the OKRs with your boss yourself — it can't sit in that meeting for you. And whatever target you set, you're the one carrying it.

</details>

<a id="skill-169"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>169. Year-End Self-Review</b> —— your self-review says "follows instructions, gets along with colleagues" — HR reads it and gives you a…</summary>

Your self-review says "follows instructions, gets along with colleagues" — HR reads it and gives you a "needs improvement."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. List your year's projects and data for Muse: "Rewrite in STAR format: situation, task, action, result with numbers."

2. Have it write the "weaknesses and improvements" part: "One real problem plus one fix already underway — not 'too much of a perfectionist.'"

3. Have it compress it to one page — "cut every meaningless platitude" — then read it once yourself for flow.

**Real talk:** Don't invent achievements — the data is on you to keep honest. It only helps with expression. If it falls apart in a background check, it's your name on it.

</details>

<a id="skill-170"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>170. Cross-Department Favor Emails</b> —— you need another team's help, your email reads stiff, they leave you on read — and the project stalls…</summary>

You need another team's help, your email reads stiff, they leave you on read — and the project stalls on your desk.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the background: "Write an ask email — open with what's in it for them, close with a clear deadline."

2. Have it draft a follow-up for "when they say no" — "swap 'could you' for 'here are the three things I need from you.'"

3. Before sending, tell it the recipient's rank: "The version for a director and the version for a frontline engineer should sound different."

**Real talk:** You send the email yourself — it can't. And if you never do anyone favors, politeness won't save you when you need one.

</details>

<a id="skill-171"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>171. Meeting Time Conflicts</b> —— three meetings at the same time, none of them optional — which one you attend is purely vibes.</summary>

Three meetings at the same time, none of them optional — which one you attend is purely vibes.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the three meetings' topics and attendees: "Rank them by 'who will yell at you if you skip' — give me a trade-off recommendation."

2. Have it write the bail-out message for the ones you drop: "Add 'I'll grab the notes after' — makes you look like you care."

3. Have it make you an anti-collision rule: "No meetings Tuesday and Thursday mornings" — and set it in your calendar.

**Real talk:** You send the "I can't make it" message yourself — it can't. And the calendar you fix yourself, too.

</details>

<a id="skill-172"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>172. Replying to Blame-Shifting Emails</b> —— you're CC'd on a blame-shifting email, the blame mysteriously lands on you — too harsh and you make…</summary>

You're CC'd on a blame-shifting email, the blame mysteriously lands on you — too harsh and you make enemies, stay silent and you eat the loss.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the original email (scrubbed of sensitive bits): "Map the timeline — where exactly did it go wrong?"

2. Have it write the reply: "Facts and timeline only, not one accusatory word — end with 'how do we fix it next.'"

3. Before sending, have it check: "Is there any line that could be screenshotted out of context?" — if yes, change it.

**Real talk:** You hit send yourself — it can't. It doesn't understand office politics; the final tone is your call.

</details>

<a id="skill-173"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>173. Papering Requirement Changes</b> —— requirements changed eight times, launch goes wrong, and the boss says "I never said that" —…</summary>

Requirements changed eight times, launch goes wrong, and the boss says "I never said that" — you have no defense.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Every time requirements change, tell Muse what changed: "Log it as a change record: when, who asked, what changed, how many schedule days it costs."

2. Have it generate the confirmation line — "you copy it into the group chat: 'Requirements now XX, schedule pushed 3 days — confirming?'"

3. At month-end have it summarize: "How many changes this month, how much schedule eaten" — and bring it to the retrospective.

**Real talk:** You send the confirmation message to the group yourself — it can't. And you have to log the change every time — it can't remember what you never told it.

</details>

<a id="skill-174"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>174. Handover Docs in a Flash</b> —— transferring or leaving, your handover doc is half-done — and the person taking over @s you every day…</summary>

Transferring or leaving, your handover doc is half-done — and the person taking over @s you every day with questions.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. List the systems you own for Muse: "Generate a handover doc skeleton: where the architecture diagrams are, the account list, the common pitfalls, the contacts."

2. You dictate section by section, it organizes them into prose — "even lines like 'never touch this, touching it breaks everything' go in."

3. Have the successor walk through it once — "mark wherever they get stuck" — you fill the gaps, then it's final.

**Real talk:** Don't give it your account passwords to keep. You send the handover doc to your successor yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-175"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>175. Onboarding a New Hire</b> —— the new hire is three days in and still setting up their environment — you can't drop your own work, and…</summary>

The new hire is three days in and still setting up their environment — you can't drop your own work, and mentoring is wearing you out.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the new hire's role: "Generate a two-week ramp-up checklist — week one: environment and docs; week two: a small task to practice on."

2. Have it write daily check-in questions — like "can you run the project yet?" — and you spend 10 minutes a day going through them.

3. After two weeks, have the new hire write their own retrospective — "where did you get stuck the longest" — and you bake that into the checklist.

**Real talk:** Mentoring is on you in person — it can't answer their questions for you. The checklist just saves you some worry; it doesn't make you hands-free.

</details>

<a id="skill-176"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>176. What to Talk About in 1v1s</b> —— you sit down for a 1v1 with your boss and just give a status update — walk out feeling like it never…</summary>

You sit down for a 1v1 with your boss and just give a status update — walk out feeling like it never happened.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse what's been on your mind: "Prep 3 questions — ask my boss for resources, feedback, and direction. Don't just report."

2. Have it play your boss's likely questions — like "what's been hard for you lately" — and get your answers ready.

3. After the chat, send it the highlights: "Turn them into action items" — and pull them out before the next 1v1 to check progress.

**Real talk:** You show up for the 1v1 yourself — it can't sit in that chair for you. Things like asking for resources: if an email can't do it, you say it face to face.

</details>

<a id="skill-177"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>177. Asking for a Raise</b> —— Booked three meetings with your boss and froze on the first sentence…</summary>

You booked three meetings with your boss to ask for a raise, froze on the first sentence, and the conversation somehow turned into team-bonding chat.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse your year's wins plus market salary data: "Write me an opener that states 'what I'm worth and why' in 30 seconds."

2. Have it prep counters for your boss's three classic deflections — "no budget," "wait a bit," "you're still young" — with a comeback for each.

3. Do a dry run: send it what you plan to say and have it play the boss and poke holes. Don't walk in until you're not nervous.

**Real talk:** Look up market salary numbers on job sites yourself — its figures are ballpark. And you still have to walk in and say it yourself. It can't open its mouth for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-178"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>178. Time-Off Requests</b> —— Your leave request reads like an essay, your manager keeps probing for details…</summary>

Your leave request reads like a little essay, your manager keeps probing for details, and the more you write the guiltier you feel.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse how many days and what kind: "Write me a leave request in three lines: which dates, who's covering my work, my emergency contact."

2. Have it make you a handoff checklist template — "you fill in names and drop it in the group chat so you look responsible."

3. Got annual leave about to expire? Have it remind you: "You still have 5 unused days — use them or lose them."

**Real talk:** You still file the request in the company OA system yourself — it can't get in there. And don't invent excuses; getting caught is worse.

</details>

<a id="skill-179"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>179. Overtime Ledger</b> —— You track overtime in your head, and at month-end your boss says "you barely…</summary>

You track your overtime in your head. When you ask for comp time at month-end, your boss says "you didn't work that much overtime, did you" — and you're speechless.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Every time you work late, tell Muse: "Log this — date, hours, what I did."

2. At month-end have it total everything up: "37 overtime hours this month — how many comp days is that per company policy?"

3. Have it draft the comp-time request for you — "you copy it and take it to your boss with data to back you up."

**Real talk:** You have to log the overtime yourself every time — it can't magically know you stayed late. And the request itself? Still on you to make.

</details>

<a id="skill-180"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>180. Commute Audit</b> —— Two hours of commuting a day, all spent doomscrolling, and you arrive as tired…</summary>

Two hours of commuting every day, all spent doomscrolling short videos, and you arrive at the office just as tired as if you hadn't rested.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your commute length: "Build me a commute lineup — industry podcasts on Mondays, Friday work reviews on Tuesdays."

2. When an idea hits you mid-commute, dictate it and send the transcript to it — "it'll file it as a to-do or an idea for you."

3. Every Friday have it work from your week's notes: "Write a 200-word recap of what my commute time actually accomplished this week."

**Real talk:** You have to transcribe the voice notes and send them yourself. And stop answering work messages on the commute — your body is yours.

</details>

<a id="skill-181"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>181. Pomodoro Scheduling</b> —— You were slammed all day, then realized at night you did nothing real…</summary>

You were slammed all day, then did the nightly review and realized you didn't touch a single real task — just answered messages.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Each morning send Muse your 3 real tasks: "Lay these out as pomodoros, hard ones in the morning, 25 minutes each."

2. Have it set the rule: "No phone during a pomodoro — messages wait for the 5-minute breaks."

3. At night report back: "Which pomodoros died and why" — so tomorrow's schedule can dodge the traps.

**Real talk:** You still have to set the timer and do it yourself — it can't watch over you. And don't lie to it about the dead pomodoros; honest logs are the only kind that help.

</details>

<a id="skill-182"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>182. Deep Work Protection</b> —— The moment you get into flow, meetings, @s, and calls shred your day…</summary>

The moment you get into flow, meetings, @mentions, and calls take turns showing up — your day gets shredded into confetti.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse what you work on: "Find the best 2-hour deep-work window in my day and block it on my calendar."

2. Have it write an auto-reply: "'I'm in deep work, back after 12' — you set it as your status."

3. Check in weekly: "How many deep-work hours did I protect this week?" If the answer's bad, have it reshuffle.

**Real talk:** You set the calendar block and the status yourself — it can't touch your schedule. If a meeting collides, you still have to go negotiate it yourself.

</details>
<a id="skill-183"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>183. Email Batching</b> —— You reply to every email the second it lands, get interrupted 20 times…</summary>

You reply to every email the second it lands, get interrupted 20 times a day, and never move the important stuff forward.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Set the rule with Muse: "I only process email at 11am and 5pm. I don't look at it otherwise."

2. Forward it your backlog: "Sort into 'reply / do / archive' — and draft replies for the ones I need to answer."

3. Have it write a signature line: "'I batch email twice a day — call for anything urgent.' Fewer pointless interruptions."

**Real talk:** You have to forward the emails yourself — it can't see your inbox. And real emergencies don't wait for batch time; just reply.

</details>

<a id="skill-184"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>184. Group Chat Digests</b> —— Your work chat does 500 messages a day. You scroll for ages and still miss…</summary>

Your work chat does 500 messages a day. You scroll until your eyes blur — and still miss the important announcements.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Copy the chat log and send it to Muse (scrubbed): "Boil this down to 5 lines: who decided what, who owes what."

2. Have it pull out the @-you parts: "List them separately — don't bury them in the chat noise."

3. Make it a ritual: "9am reads yesterday's, pre-logoff reads today's." Build the habit.

**Real talk:** You copy and send the logs yourself — it can't get into your WeChat. Scrub anything sensitive before you send it.

</details>

<a id="skill-185"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>185. Action Item Tracking</b> —— Everyone nods in the meeting, nobody follows up, and next week you rehash…</summary>

Everyone nods in the meeting, nobody follows up on the action items, and next week you're rehashing the exact same thing.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send the meeting notes to Muse: "Pull out every action item in the format: who, what, due when."

2. The day before a deadline, have it remind you: "Xiao Li's proposal is due today — go nudge him."

3. Every Friday have it summarize: "Which action items are overdue this week" — post it in the group for all to see.

**Real talk:** The nudging messages go out with your thumbs — it can't @ your coworkers. And you have to pull the action items out of the notes yourself each time.

</details>

<a id="skill-186"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>186. Project Post-Mortems</b> —— Project ends, everyone scatters, and you step in the same potholes next time…</summary>

Project ends, everyone scatters, and you step in the same potholes next time — because the retro turned into a pat-on-the-back session.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse how the project went: "Organize it as '3 things we did well, 3 potholes' — not a play-by-play."

2. Have it turn each pothole into a "next time do this": e.g. "'QA must be in the requirements review.'"

3. Have it save it as a template: "Read this when the next project kicks off" — don't file the retro and forget it.

**Real talk:** You run the retro meeting yourself — it can't host for you. Somebody has to be brave enough to tell the truth; the template just helps you remember it.

</details>

<a id="skill-187"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>187. Incident Reports</b> —— Production broke, you fixed it, and the writeup sat untouched for two weeks…</summary>

Production broke, you fixed it, and the incident writeup sat untouched for two weeks with nobody writing it.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the incident timeline: "Format it as a report: symptoms, root cause, fix process, impact duration."

2. Have it write the follow-ups: "Each with an owner and a deadline — no empty 'improve monitoring' fluff."

3. Have it scan the draft: "Flag any blame-shifting sentences" — rewrite those to target the problem, not the person.

**Real talk:** The timeline comes from you — it has no idea what happened on your servers. And the report goes out under your name; it can't send it for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-188"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>188. Weekly Standup in 3 Lines</b> —— Your turn in the weekly meeting comes, nerves hit, and you ramble…</summary>

Your turn in the weekly meeting comes, nerves hit, and you ramble through five minutes of play-by-play while everyone stares at their phones.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse what you did this week: "Compress it into three lines: what I did, where I'm stuck, what's next."

2. Have it prep a "if they ask" version: "Data and details live here — stay silent unless asked."

3. Run through it 10 minutes early: "Cut if it runs long" — rehearse until it's under a minute.

**Real talk:** You still walk into that meeting yourself — it can't speak for you. And don't save blockers only for the meeting; DM the relevant people beforehand.

</details>
<a id="skill-189"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>189. English Meeting → Chinese Minutes</b> —— You strained to follow the whole English meeting, then got asked…</summary>

You strained to follow the whole English meeting, then got asked to write up Chinese minutes — and your brain went blank.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the meeting transcription (scrubbed): "Turn it into Chinese minutes, split into decisions, to-dos, and open disputes."

2. Have it flag the parts you didn't catch: "Listen to the replay for these lines — don't guess."

3. Before sending the minutes, have it check: "Did I translate 'might' as 'definitely' anywhere?" That kind of word is the real trap.

**Real talk:** You transcribe the recording yourself — it can't listen to audio. And for sensitive meetings, check first whether your company even allows recording.

</details>

<a id="skill-190"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>190. Vendor Contract Renewals</b> —— A vendor contract auto-renewed for a year, price up 30%, and you just…</summary>

A vendor contract auto-renewed for a year, price up 30%, and you only just noticed.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Snap photos of the contract's key pages and send them to Muse: "Extract the expiry date, renewal terms, and price-hike clause into one table."

2. Have it ping you 60 days before expiry: "And list negotiation ammo: competitors' quotes, your usage volume."

3. After negotiating, send it the new contract: "Compare against the old one — check for any sneaky new clauses."

**Real talk:** You negotiate yourself — it can't haggle for you. You sign the contract yourself too; it can only read the fine print, not own it.

</details>

<a id="skill-191"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>191. Comparing 3 Vendor Quotes</b> —— Three vendors, three different quote formats, and in the end you pick…</summary>

Three vendors' quotes come in three different formats. You compare until it's all gut feel — and get questioned after you pick.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse all three quotes: "Build a comparison table — features as rows, vendors as columns, missing items flagged."

2. Have it compute total cost of ownership: "Don't just look at the quote — add implementation fees and annual service."

3. Have it write the selection memo: "Recommend A, three reasons, one risk" — you take that to the meeting.

**Real talk:** You get the quotes from the vendors yourself — it can't request them. And the final call is yours; if you pick wrong, the blame lands on you.

</details>

<a id="skill-192"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>192. Budget Request Justifications</b> —— Your budget writeup was bone-dry, and one "do we really need this"…</summary>

Your budget writeup was bone-dry, and one "do we really need this" from the boss sent it back to the shredder.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse what you want to buy and why: "Write the request — lead with the cost of NOT spending, then the payoff of spending."

2. Have it prep three tiers: "Basic, standard, deluxe, 30% apart — so the boss has something to choose."

3. Have it roleplay the boss's objections: "'Can't we just use the free one?' — rehearse your answer" in advance.

**Real talk:** You file the request in OA yourself — it can't get into the system. And don't fabricate numbers; one question from the boss and you're exposed.

</details>

<a id="skill-193"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>193. Internal Talk Slide Outlines</b> —— Your turn for the internal share came up and you made a 40-slide…</summary>

Your turn for the internal share came up and you made a 40-slide deck, all text — nobody was listening by slide 10.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the topic: "Build a 15-slide outline, one line per slide, conclusion first."

2. Have it find you 3 stories or cases: "Dry lecturing loses people — stories keep them."

3. After the talk, send it the audience's questions: "Organize them into an FAQ" — reuse it next time.

**Real talk:** You build the slides yourself — it can only give you the outline. You're the one on stage; if you're nervous, rehearse twice.

</details>

<a id="skill-194"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>194. Pitfall Playbooks</b> —— You stepped in the same pothole twice in six months. "I'll write it down next…</summary>

You stepped in the same pothole twice in six months. Every time you think "I'll write it down next time" — and next time you forget again.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse what happened: "Write it up as a playbook — context, what the trap was, how I climbed out, what to do next time."

2. Have it pick a findable title: "Not 'retro' — something like 'Payment callback timeouts: 3-step triage.'"

3. Post it to the team wiki: "@ the relevant people to read it" — don't let it rot on your laptop.

**Real talk:** You post to the wiki yourself — it can't publish. The @-ing people part is on you too.

</details>
<a id="skill-195"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>195. Shortcut Cheat Sheets</b> —— The software you use daily, and you only know copy-paste. Your mouse…</summary>

The software you use every day, and the only shortcuts you know are copy-paste. Your efficiency is being carried by brute-force mouse work.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your 3 most-used apps: "For each, list the 15 highest-frequency shortcuts, ranked by how often they're used."

2. Have it lay them out as a cheat sheet: "Print it and tape it to your monitor — rotate a new batch weekly."

3. Learn 5 new ones a week: "Have it quiz you: 'How do I quickly do X in Y?'" Miss one, drill it again.

**Real talk:** You print the cheat sheet yourself — it can't do that. And shortcuts aren't about memorizing; they're about using.

</details>

<a id="skill-196"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>196. File Naming Conventions</b> —— The team drive is full of "final", "final-2", "final-FOR-REAL". Finding…</summary>

The team drive is full of "final," "final-2," "final-FOR-REAL." Finding anything is pure guesswork.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your file types: "Set a naming convention — date plus project plus version, like 20261005-website-v3."

2. Have it write a 50-word explainer: "Pin it at the drive root — new hires read this first."

3. Give it the list of messy filenames: "Batch-rename them per the convention" — back up before you run it.

**Real talk:** The renaming is done by your hands — it can't touch your drive. And once the convention's set, someone has to enforce it.

</details>

<a id="skill-197"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>197. Reinstall Checklists</b> —— Reinstall the OS and spend a whole day reinstalling apps and re-tuning…</summary>

Reinstall the OS and spend a whole day reinstalling apps and re-tuning configs — a full day gone and you're still not back to working state.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse what you do: "Make a reinstall checklist — apps, plugins, config files, in install order."

2. Have it spell out the backup steps: "Editor settings sync, browser bookmark export — step by step."

3. Check things off as you reinstall: "Anything missing jumps out at you" — then update the checklist for next time.

**Real talk:** The reinstalling and configuring is all you — it can't touch your machine. And back up the important stuff three times before you wipe.

</details>

<a id="skill-198"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>198. Background Check Prep</b> —— The new company wants a background check, and you're scrambling for…</summary>

The new company wants a background check, and you're scrambling for references and employment letters — nearly delaying your start date.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Give Muse the list of required materials: "Make a prep checklist — separation letter, pay stubs, references — check them off one by one."

2. Have it draft the heads-up message for your former boss: "'A company may contact you for a background check' — give them advance notice."

3. Have it remind you: "Which materials are confidential, which can be redacted" — don't fire off everything unedited.

**Real talk:** The paperwork is on you — it can't issue those documents. And you call your references yourself; it can't make that call for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-199"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>199. Planning the Office Party Act</b> —— You got volunteered for the annual party act, the whole team's…</summary>

You got volunteered for the annual party act, the whole team's counting on you, and all you can say is "how about we all sing something."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse your team size and vibe: "Give me 3 act ideas, each with duration, headcount, and a props list."

2. Have it write the emcee script and the run-of-show: "Who goes when, how to rescue a flop — all in one table."

3. Rehearse once, note the failure points: "Hand them to it to fix the plan" — don't save the firefighting for the stage.

**Real talk:** The rehearsing and the performing are on you — it can't go on stage for you. And don't go overboard; safety first.

</details>

<a id="skill-200"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>200. Team-Building Plans</b> —— Team building is dinner-and-KTV again. Everyone's on their phones, and you…</summary>

Team building is dinner-and-KTV again. Everyone's on their phones, and you're the one getting roasted for "no originality."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse the headcount, budget, and average age: "Give me 3 team-building plans, at least one indoor and one outdoor."

2. Have it build the budget: "Venue, food, transport itemized — stay under budget."

3. Have it write the announcement: "Time, place, meetup point, all spelled out — so nobody has to ask you one by one."

**Real talk:** You book the venue yourself — it can't do that. The announcement goes out from your account too; it can't send it.

</details>

---

## 🏠 Everyday Life

> Daily chores: reminders, documents, deliveries, home stuff

<a id="skill-201"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>201. ID Expiry Reminders</b> —— Found out your ID was expired at the bank counter — wasted trip, then weeks waiting on a replacement…</summary>

Found out your ID was expired at the bank counter — wasted trip, and the replacement takes another two weeks.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "Remember these: my national ID expires May 2031, passport September 2028, driver's license March 2027."

2. Set the rule: "Remind me 90 days, 30 days, and 7 days before each one expires."

3. When you renew one, tell it the new date and it'll keep watching the next cycle.

**Real talk:** You have to report the dates yourself — it can't see your ID. It just yells when the time comes; the renewal errand is still yours.

</details>

<a id="skill-202"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>202. Countdowns for Important Dates</b> —— Forgot your anniversary last year — heard about it for three straight months…</summary>

Forgot your anniversary last year and heard about it for three straight months. Not happening again this year.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "Remember these dates: our anniversary May 20, Mom's birthday November 8, my buddy's wedding December 25."

2. Set the rule: "Remind me 14 days before each one to get a gift, again 3 days before, and first thing in the morning on the day itself."

3. No gift ideas? Ask it: "Mom's turning 60, budget 2,000 yuan — suggest a few can't-miss gifts." It'll build options around your budget.

**Real talk:** You report the dates; you buy the gift and say the words yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-203"></a>

<details>
