2. Say: "Sort into bugs, suggestions, praise — flag the top five most mentioned."

3. Have it draft the release notes from that — edit and ship.

**Real talk:** Exporting is on you. Which ones are actually fixed versus promised — you set that story; it just organizes.

</details>

<a id="skill-460"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>460. Turn stars and issues into a weekly open-source report</b> —— Stars up or down — pure vibes. Writing the report, you can't say anything concrete.</summary>

Stars up or down — pure vibes. Writing the weekly report, you flip around and can't say anything concrete.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse this week's star count plus new issue and PR links.

2. Say: "Build a weekly report: what moved, what users are asking, what to fix next week."

3. Add your two cents — post it in the group or pin it to the README discussion.

**Real talk:** You read the numbers off GitHub yourself and tell it. It doesn't crawl your repo automatically.

</details>

<a id="skill-461"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>461. Feed support chats back into the reply playbook</b> —— Built the reply playbook, then it gathered dust — every new weird question answered on the fly.</summary>

Built the reply playbook, then it gathered dust. Every new weird question gets answered on the fly, from scratch.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Weekly, screenshot the Xianyu (China's secondhand marketplace) support chats and send them to Muse.

2. Say: "Against my current playbook, find the new questions it doesn't cover — write three new replies."

3. Keep the ones you like — the playbook grows itself weekly.

**Real talk:** Xianyu gives it no API — you screenshot the chats. And sending the reply to the buyer is still your finger.

</details>

<a id="skill-462"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>462. Turn research summaries into a take library</b> —— The report made sense when you read it — two weeks later, not one take comes back.</summary>

The research report made sense when you read it. Two weeks later when you need it, not a single take comes back to you.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Collect the summaries you had Muse condense, and send them over regularly.

2. Say: "Distill each one's core call, filed by sector — a take library."

3. Before writing or deciding, ask it first: anything in the library on this? Did the old call hold up?

**Real talk:** The takes are secondhand material you fed it. Citing data in a real report — verify against the original yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-463"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>463. Turn screenshots into big-print tutorials for your parents</b> —— Teaching your parents the phone over the phone — three tries later, they still call back.</summary>

Teaching your parents to use their phone over the phone — three explanations later, they still call back next time.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Screenshot each step of the process, screen by screen, for Muse.

2. Say: "In screenshot order, write a big-print tutorial — one action per step."

3. Print it or drop it in the family group chat — they just follow the taps.

**Real talk:** Screenshotting every step fully is on you. Their phone model isn't yours — double-check the button positions yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-464"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>464. Turn organized photos into a family yearbook</b> —— Photos are sorted by event — but they just lie there neatly, nobody opens them twice.</summary>

The photos are sorted by event — but they just lie there neatly. Nobody opens them a second time.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the year's photos by event — have it pick the few dozen with the most story.

2. Say: "Write a 50-word caption per event, strung into a year-long story."

3. Add names and the embarrassing bits yourself — make it an e-album for the family group.

**Real talk:** It doesn't know the names or the embarrassing bits — you fill those in. It just picks and strings.

</details>

<a id="skill-465"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>465. Let the weekly review write next week's plan</b> —— Sunday night, planning next week — staring at a blank doc, pure vibes.</summary>

Sunday night, planning next week. Staring at a blank doc, going purely on vibes.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse this week's calendar and to-do completion.

2. Say: "Review first: which three got done, which two slipped, where did the time go."

3. Then have it plan next week from the review: slipped items first, breathing room between meetings.

**Real talk:** You export the data yourself. It plans — executing is on you.

</details>

<a id="skill-466"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>466. JD + résumé: score the match before rewriting</b> —— One résumé for every application — HR says you're not a fit, and you don't know why.</summary>

One résumé for every application. HR says you're not a fit, and you don't know where you fall short.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the target role's job description (JD) plus your résumé.

2. Say: "Score it: what's the match percentage, which keywords am I missing?"

3. Have it rewrite around the missing keywords — same experience, phrased toward the role.

**Real talk:** It rewrites phrasing, not experience. Don't have it invent things you never did — you can't survive the background check.

</details>

<a id="skill-467"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>467. Turn the year's bills into a tax-deduction checklist</b> —— Tax season arrives — mortgage, elder-care deductions, and you kept receipts for none of it.</summary>

Tax filing season arrives and you remember: mortgage, supporting-elderly-parents deductions — you kept receipts for none of it.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Send Muse the year's bank statements and big-expense receipts.

2. Say: "Against the six special additional deduction categories, which do I qualify for, and what paperwork am I missing?"

3. Fill the gaps from the checklist, then enter them one by one at filing.

**Real talk:** Keeping the receipts is on you. Its math is a reference — the numbers you file are your responsibility.

</details>

<a id="skill-468"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>468. Calendar + to-dos: plan the day every morning</b> —— Calendar and to-dos don't talk — mornings, no idea what goes first.</summary>

Calendar and to-dos don't talk to each other. Mornings, no idea what goes first — the day ends in busy nothing.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Have Muse read your calendar and your to-do list at once.

2. Say: "See the gaps between meetings, slot the to-dos by urgency — make me today's timeline."

3. Evenings, have it reconcile: what percent of the plan got done, roll the rest to tomorrow.

**Real talk:** Both need your authorization first. Surprises don't fit its plan — you reshuffle those yourself.

</details>

<a id="skill-469"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>469. Turn takeout orders into a monthly food-and-drink bill</b> —— Takeout, order by order — month-end, food and drink ate a third of your salary.</summary>

Takeout, order by order, feels like nothing. Month-end: food and drink ate a third of your salary.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. End of each month, screenshot the takeout app's monthly bill for Muse.

2. Say: "Tally by category: how much on meals, bubble tea, late-night snacks — and my top three most-ordered spots."

3. Have it set a "next-month takeout budget line" — over it, and it's home cooking.

**Real talk:** Screenshotting the bill is you, in the app. It can only do the math — it can't hold your hand back.

</details>

<a id="skill-470"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>470. Turn flight + hotel bookings into a packing list</b> —— Flights and hotel booked — packing the night before, the charger always misses the bag.</summary>

Flights and hotel booked. Packing the night before, the charger or the toothbrush always misses the bag.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Screenshot the flight and hotel bookings for Muse — have it pull times, cities, days.

2. Say: "From the destination weather and trip length, make a packing list: documents, clothes, electronics."

3. Check things off the day before — anything missing, there's still time to buy.

**Real talk:** It checks public weather data — double-check extreme weather yourself. Whether the documents made the bag is on you.

</details>

---

## 💡 Honest Limits

> What Muse can't do — let's be upfront

<a id="skill-471"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>471. It can't get into your banking or brokerage apps</b> —— People ask: "Can it just operate my bank app — transfer, trade stocks?"</summary>

People keep asking: "Can it just operate my bank app — transfer money, trade stocks?" The answer is no — and don't trust anyone who says yes.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Remember the iron rule: anything moving money, Muse analyzes — never touches.

2. Send it statement screenshots: "Calculate this year's returns for me, by quarter."

3. You tap the actual transfers and trades in the app yourself — it double-checks the numbers beside you.

**Real talk:** This isn't a tech limitation — it's a safety red line. AI touching your money directly is high-risk by definition.

</details>

<a id="skill-472"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>472. Great memory — but don't use it as your password vault</b> —— Muse remembers everything, so people paste passwords in and say "remember this."</summary>

Muse remembers everything, so people paste their passwords in and say "remember this for me." That's extremely dangerous.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Passwords, keys, verification codes — never send them to any AI, Muse included.

2. When you need it to log you into a site, say: "Walk me through the official authorization flow" — never paste a password in chat.

3. If one slips out, change it immediately — assume it's already leaked.

**Real talk:** Muse's memory serves you, but chat logs, screenshots, and forwards can all spread secrets. Passwords belong in a password manager, and nowhere else.

</details>

<a id="skill-473"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>473. You have to tap "buy" on tickets yourself</b> —— Everyone wants an AI to guard tickets for the New Year rush — but "buy and pay for me" is where it stops.</summary>

For the Chinese New Year travel rush, everyone wants an AI to guard tickets for them. But "buy and pay for me" is where Muse stops.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Tell Muse: "Watch this train's remaining tickets and the waitlist for me — shout the second there's a seat."

2. Fill in the waitlist order and passenger info in 12306 (China's railway ticketing platform) ahead of time.

3. The alert lands, you tap in and pay yourself — the whole thing takes under a minute.

**Real talk:** Watching tickets is grunt work — all its. Ordering is accountability work — all yours. Get on the waitlist first; it beats hammering refresh by a mile.

</details>

<a id="skill-474"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>474. Health reads are reference, not diagnosis</b> —— Lab report full of arrows — can't read it, don't want to queue for a doctor.</summary>

Your lab report is full of arrows. You can't read it and don't want to queue for a doctor, so you want AI to just tell you "am I sick?"

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Photograph the report: "Translate the abnormal markers into plain English for me."

2. Then ask: "What are these usually tied to — what should I focus on at the next checkup?"

3. Take its organized version to your doctor — the conversation goes twice as fast.

**Real talk:** It can help you read the report — it can't diagnose, prescribe, or set a treatment plan. On body stuff, AI advises; the doctor decides.

</details>

<a id="skill-475"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>475. Real-time data lags — trust the official source</b> —— You ask "where's this flight now" or "what's this stock at" — it gives a number, and you believe it.</summary>

You ask "where's this flight right now" or "what's this stock at" — it gives you a number, and you believe it.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Use its flight statuses and stock prices as a reference — get a rough read first.

2. Before deciding (rebooking, trading), re-check the live status in the official app.

3. Tell it: "Watch this flight for me — ping me if the delay passes an hour." Watching changes is fine; as a tick-level terminal, no.

**Real talk:** Its data lags. For split-second stuff like flights and stock prices, check the live page in the official app yourself before deciding — don't take its word alone.

</details>

<a id="skill-476"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>476. It won't fight your battles, borrow money, or vouch for you</b> —— People want Muse to go argue, dun, or borrow in their place — even impersonate them.</summary>

People want Muse to go argue with someone, dun them, borrow money — even impersonate them.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Remember: anything needing your name, your liability, your face — Muse drafts, never sends.

2. For dunning, apologies, negotiations: "Draft me a version — firm but polite." You review, you send.

3. Any "pretend to be me" / "don't let them tell it's AI" request — take a different approach.

**Real talk:** AI is your advisor and your secretary, not your stunt double. Anything needing a person and their credibility — that's you, in person.

</details>

<a id="skill-477"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>477. It can't help you inside the exam hall</b> —— People want Muse to feed them answers mid-exam, thinking nobody will know.</summary>

People want Muse to feed them answers mid-exam, thinking nobody will ever know.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: feeding answers for cheating is help it won't give. That's a red line.

2. If you actually want to pass, send the textbook and past papers: "Highlight the key points, then build me three mock exams."

3. Before the test, have it quiz you like an examiner — patch the weak spots on the spot. Way more reliable than cheating.

**Real talk:** Caught cheating, the record is yours. It can prep you for the exam — it can't sit it for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-478"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>478. It can't compute lottery numbers</b> —— People keep asking "what are next draw's numbers," hoping the AI has insider info.</summary>

People keep asking "what are next draw's numbers," hoping the AI has insider info.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, accept: random draws have no pattern. It can't compute them — anyone who says they can is a scammer.

2. If you really want to play: "Calculate the win probability and expected return for each game type."

3. Once you see the expected value, it's clear: lottery tickets are a tax, not an investment.

**Real talk:** If it could predict the lottery, it wouldn't be here chatting with you. Guard your own wallet.

</details>

<a id="skill-479"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>479. It can't delete what other people posted</b> —— Someone hung you out to dry in a post — you want Muse to "hack in" and delete it.</summary>

Someone hung you out to dry in a post, and you want Muse to "hack in" and delete it. Done and dusted.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: it has no admin rights — it can't delete anything anyone else posted.

2. Send it the post link: "Draft me a factual complaint and rebuttal."

3. You file the platform report or contact the poster yourself — the draft's ready, the action's yours.

**Real talk:** Takedowns go through platform process — you operate that yourself. It writes drafts; it doesn't play hacker.

</details>

<a id="skill-480"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>480. You have to show up in court yourself</b> —— In a dispute, people want AI to write a defense and call it "full representation."</summary>

In a dispute, people want AI to write a defense statement and call it "full representation."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: it's not a lawyer — it can't represent you in court, sign, or give depositions.

2. Send it the case materials: "Lay out the timeline, flag the evidence points in my favor."

3. Take its organized file to a real lawyer — the consultation fees you save are pure profit.

**Real talk:** The court recognizes you and your lawyer. It's your desk-side advisor, nothing more.

</details>

<a id="skill-481"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>481. The signature has to be yours</b> —— Contracts, authorizations, guarantee letters — people want AI to "just sign for me."</summary>

Contracts, authorizations, guarantee letters — people want AI to "just sign for me" and skip the trip.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: a signature means you own it. Nobody can do that motion for you.

2. Before signing, send it the document: "Flag the clauses that bind me once signed."

3. Sign once you understand it, file the scan, and have it archive and remind you of follow-up dates.

**Real talk:** The pen drops, the liability is yours. It can help you understand — it can't put pen to paper for you.

</details>

<a id="skill-482"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>482. What it says isn't always right either</b> —— It answers with so much confidence you forget it can bullshit with a straight face.</summary>

It answers with so much confidence you forget it can also bullshit with a straight face.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Remember: fluent doesn't mean correct — numbers, names, policy clauses deserve extra skepticism.

2. On important stuff: "Cite sources — the kind I can click and verify."

3. On money and health conclusions, cross-check once before acting.

**Real talk:** It hallucinates sometimes — you're the last line of defense. A quick verify-before-you-trust is your habit to build.

</details>

<a id="skill-483"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>483. You have to date for yourself</b> —— People want Muse to chat for them, ghostwrite love letters, even take their blind dates.</summary>

People want Muse to chat for them, ghostwrite love letters, even go on blind dates in their place.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: the most valuable thing in romance is a real person — the moment it talks, it smells like customer service.

2. Before a date: "Give me three small-talk topics that don't die." It can advise on what to wear too.

3. You can send it chat logs to review — but the messages go out from you.

**Real talk:** Butterflies can't be outsourced. The other person likes you — not your AI ghostwriter.

</details>

<a id="skill-484"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>484. It can't get into other people's accounts</b> —— "Can you log into my partner's WeChat and take a look?" — asked with a straight face.</summary>

"Can you log into my partner's WeChat and take a look for me?" — asked with a completely straight face.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: cracking, stealing, bypassing verification — it does none of it. That's a legal red line.

2. Forgot a password? Go through official recovery: "Walk me through this platform's appeal process."

3. Turn on two-factor for the accounts that matter — have it list the ones still missing it.

**Real talk:** Other people's accounts are off-limits no matter how close you are. Your own accounts — the verification step is yours too.

</details>

<a id="skill-485"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>485. It won't help rig votes</b> —— Class votes, company awards — people want AI to mass-vote and "give it a push."</summary>

Class votes for the kids, company awards — people want AI to mass-vote and "give it a push."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: vote-rigging is cheating. Get caught and it's your face on the line.

2. If you actually want to win: "Write me a decent campaign pitch — make the case for why me."

3. Post it where it belongs, campaign openly. Losing with dignity beats winning dirty.

**Real talk:** You can lose the count; you can't lose your character. Cheating's consequences land on you.

</details>

<a id="skill-486"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>486. Forged documents are a hard no</b> —— "Just this once" — a fake certificate, a tweaked ID-photo background.</summary>

People want a fake certificate, a tweaked ID-photo background — "just this once."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: forged IDs, seals, certificates — it touches none of it. Don't even ask; it's illegal.

2. Proper reissue feels like a hassle: "Look up the reissue process and what to bring for this document."

3. Bring everything on the list once — one trip and it's done, beats looking over your shoulder.

**Real talk:** Fake papers get caught, and it's you who goes in. It'll point you to the legit path — it doesn't walk the crooked one.

</details>

<a id="skill-487"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>487. It can't move a single cent for you</b> —— People think AI is omnipotent — "transfer this to my landlord for me," just like that.</summary>

People think AI is omnipotent — "transfer this to my landlord for me," just like that.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: it can't touch your money. Transfers, repayments, red packets — your finger, every time.

2. "Calculate how much, to whom, what the memo says — lay it out clean."

3. You open the banking app and follow the sheet — screenshot the transfer and have it log it.

**Real talk:** Money moves, your finger moves last — always. It does the math, not the cashiering.

</details>

<a id="skill-488"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>488. You still have to pick up the phone</b> —— Don't want the sales call or the HR call — want AI to "chat with them first"...</summary>

Don't want the sales call or the HR call — want AI to "chat with them first"...

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: it has no mouth. It can't take calls or wear your voice.

2. Before an important call: "Give me a call outline — what they might ask, how I answer."

3. Hang up on spam, then have it draft a template text to reply to everyone at once.

**Real talk:** Speaking up can't be outsourced. It preps the script — you take the stage.

</details>

<a id="skill-489"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>489. Your face is the one on the video call</b> —— Don't want to show your face at standup — want AI to sit there on camera "as you."</summary>

Don't want to show your face at the morning standup — want AI to sit there on camera "as you."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: it has no face. No video, and it can't field your boss's follow-ups.

2. Before the meeting: "From the agenda, prep me three lines: progress, blockers, what I need."

3. You show up and deliver — after, drop it the notes to organize. No dropped threads.

**Real talk:** The person on camera has to be you. It preps the meeting — it doesn't attend it.

</details>

<a id="skill-490"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>490. The final call is yours</b> —— Big decision, can't decide — you want AI to just tell you "pick A or B."</summary>

Big decision and you can't decide — you want AI to just tell you "pick A or B."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, accept: it gives analysis, not edicts. Pick wrong and it doesn't reimburse you.

2. "Lay out A vs B — pros, cons, risks, worst case — as a table."

3. You stare at the table and decide — then have it break the decision into steps.

**Real talk:** It's your life — you bang the gavel. It lays out the information; it doesn't take the responsibility.

</details>

<a id="skill-491"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>491. It can't read minds</b> —— "You're an AI — how do you not know this?" As if it should know everything.</summary>

"You're an AI — how do you not know this?" As if it should know everything.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, accept: what you never told it, it doesn't know. Don't expect guesses.

2. Before asking: "Here's what I do, what I'm solving, what the constraints are" — the fuller the background, the better.

3. Fuller background, better answers. Treat it like a new hire, not a god.

**Real talk:** You feed the information — you set the quality. One more line from you, half the mistakes from it.

</details>

<a id="skill-492"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>492. It won't help dox or dogpile anyone</b> —— Grudge against someone — want their info dug up, a pile-on organized, to blow off steam.</summary>

You've got a grudge against someone — want their info dug up, a pile-on organized, to blow off steam.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: digging up privacy, inciting pile-ons — it does none of it. Don't even ask; it's illegal.

2. Real dispute? Send the chat logs: "Lay out the facts for me, draft a complaint."

3. Go through platform reporting or legal channels — the materials are ready, the road is yours to walk.

**Real talk:** Venting feels good for a second; the liability is yours. It helps you argue your case — it doesn't help you hurt people.

</details>

<a id="skill-493"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>493. It can't click "buy" on your trades</b> —— Watching the market, wanting it to "auto-buy for me at this price" — a free trader.</summary>

Watching the market, wanting it to "auto-buy for me at this price" — a free trader.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: it can't log into your brokerage. Placing and canceling orders — your finger, every time.

2. "Set my alerts: what price triggers, how my phone should scream."

3. Alert lands, you open the app and act — then have it log the trade.

**Real talk:** It can't even see your positions. Money in, money out — your finger's on all of it.

</details>

<a id="skill-494"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>494. It's no fortune-teller either</b> —— Hand over your birth chart, ask about your future — thinking AI out-divines the masters.</summary>

Hand over your birth chart and ask about your future — thinking AI out-divines the masters.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, accept: fortune-telling has no scientific basis. What it says is horoscope-blogger tier — entertainment only.

2. Actually anxious? "Break my options into a pros-and-cons table."

3. Think through it line by line — beats waiting on an auspicious character.

**Real talk:** Your life's script is yours to write. At most it's a "cyber mystic" that doesn't charge.

</details>

<a id="skill-495"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>495. It can't do chores or watch the kids</b> —— Crashed on the couch, fantasizing that AI could do the dishes, mop, and put the kid to bed.</summary>

Crashed on the couch, fantasizing that AI could do the dishes, mop the floor, and put the kid to bed.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, accept: it has no hands. Nothing in the physical world is on its menu.

2. "Make me a chore roster: who does what, kid's bedtime" — stick it on the fridge.

3. Meltdown parenting moments? Have it give you three proven soothing lines — works better than yelling.

**Real talk:** The dishes are yours to wash, the kid yours to soothe. It can talk — the doing is on you.

</details>

<a id="skill-496"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>496. It won't make fake invoices</b> —— Reimbursement quota unspent — "issue a few" to use it up, figuring nobody checks.</summary>

Reimbursement quota unspent — someone wants to "issue a few" invoices to use it up, figuring nobody checks.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: fake invoices are illegal. It won't make a single one.

2. Got legit receipts unfiled? Send them over: "Organize these into an expense report."

3. Quota unspent stays unspent — have it plan how next month's spend goes further.

**Real talk:** When the tax bureau comes knocking, it's you who goes in. It saves you hassle — it doesn't help you break the law.

</details>

<a id="skill-497"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>497. Don't copy-paste its professional advice</b> —— Its legal and tax advice sounds airtight — copy it into real life, step in a hole.</summary>

Its legal and tax advice sounds airtight. Copy it into real life — step in a hole.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, accept: it's not a licensed lawyer or accountant. What it gives is general information.

2. Use its answers as a "question list" — go through them one by one with a real professional.

3. Policies change fast — when it looks up the latest clause for you, check the publish date.

**Real talk:** When things go south, you're the one holding the bag. Before big-money decisions, paying a real human is the best value.

</details>

<a id="skill-498"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>498. It won't track people for you</b> —— Want an ex's whereabouts, your kid's location — "just one look."</summary>

Want an ex's whereabouts, your kid's location — "just one look."

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: location tracking, digging up hotel records — it refuses all of it.

2. Worried about your kid? Use the phone's built-in family sharing — the setup is yours to do.

3. Relationship distrust? "Help me think through how to have a real talk about this" — beats checking up on them.

**Real talk:** Other people's whereabouts are private — cross that line and you're liable. It doesn't hand you the knife.

</details>

<a id="skill-499"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>499. Checkout is on you</b> —— The price looks great after comparing — "just order it for me," as if it held your wallet.</summary>

The price looks great after comparing — "just order it for me," as if it held your wallet.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. First, be clear: it can't get into your shopping apps. The payment tap is your finger, every time.

2. "Give me the pre-decision final check: price, coupons, shipping insurance — anything missed?"

3. You place the order, screenshot it to Muse, and have it watch logistics and price protection.

**Real talk:** Money leaves your pocket — the finger is yours. It advises; it doesn't pay.

</details>

<a id="skill-500"></a>

<details>
<summary><b>500. Smart as it is, it's still just a tool</b> —— Once it clicks, you ask it about everything — slowly handing over your own judgment too.</summary>

Once it clicks, you ask it about everything — slowly handing over your own judgment too.

**3 prompts, copy and use:**

1. Get the framing right: it's your sharpest advisor and secretary — but advisors don't give the orders.

2. "Gather intel, lay out options, break it into steps for me" — that's its home turf.

3. Deciding, signing, owning, showing up — always yours. That's where your value lives.

**Real talk:** Tools work best when the human stays the master. The clearer you are, the better it gets.

</details>
